- **31 Jul (🗂 #273 — THREE MIGRATION DIRECTORIES BECOME ONE, AND THE ITEM'S OWN PREMISE WAS WRONG):** #273 says to make `supabase/migrations/` canonical *"because it is what Vida → Engine runs"*. **It is not**, and verifying that claim was the first step rather than an afterthought. `/operator/migrations/run` calls `runPendingMigrations`, which executes **`PENDING_MIGRATIONS` — a TypeScript constant** — and `pending-migrations.ts` states exactly why it is not read from disk: **`.sql` files are not copied into `dist/` by tsc, so a file read would work locally and fail in production.** **No directory has ever been applied to anything**, and **two of the runner's own twelve entries had their file in `apps/api/src/migrations`** — so the premise was not even true of the runner's own contents. Adopting it unexamined would have produced a doc that was confidently wrong about the one mechanism that touches the database. **The split, measured:** 94 · 19 · 13, and **not a single file was in more than one directory.** So *"where is the migration that created this table?"* depended entirely on which folder you happened to open first, with nothing to tell you two more existed. **All 32 outside files copied into `supabase/migrations/` with provenance headers and BYTE-IDENTICAL bodies.** Nothing rewritten, reordered or tidied on the way in — whether a migration was applied to production is a **historical fact**, and editing it while copying destroys the only record of what actually ran. Originals kept, not deleted (rule 3), each carrying a tombstone header naming its canonical copy; a README in all three directories saying what the folder is and what to change instead. **⚠️ AND ONE MIGRATION HAD NO FILE ANYWHERE.** Mapping all twelve runner keys to disk found `20260726_campaign_copilot_columns` existing **only as a template string inside `pending-migrations.ts`** — **a statement the product can apply to production that nothing in the migration record describes.** That is #558 in its purest form, and it was invisible until the two halves were compared. Recovered verbatim; the constant remains the thing that runs. **The duplication risk is converted into a guard rather than accepted:** copying leaves two files that can drift, which is precisely the disease #273 exists to cure. `migration-home.test.ts` asserts every tombstoned body stays **identical** to its canonical copy, that every runner entry **has** a file, that all three READMEs state the distinction easiest to get wrong — **recording a migration is not running one** — and that the runner still reads a constant rather than a directory, so if somebody ever adds a directory reader the gate fails and the READMEs get rewritten. 13 tests, red-proved five ways. **⚠️ AND A BROKEN ASSERTION IDIOM IN MY OWN TEST, caught by red-proving:** I wrote `expect(a) || expect(b)` twice. **`expect` THROWS on failure, so the right-hand side is dead code** — only the first assertion ever runs, and the test reads as covering two cases while covering one. Same family as the `'hypergrowth plan'` substring that passed for the wrong reason (#549) and a comment-bound assertion that cannot fail for the reason it names: **an assertion that cannot express what it claims to check.** Rewritten explicitly. **Stale references updated, and one of them was itself a #558 in miniature:** the admin launch checklist pointed nine rows at `packages/db/src/migrations/`, and **`TECH-STACK.md` described `apps/api/src/migrations/` as "7 files" when it held 19** — a doc describing a directory that had almost tripled since anyone looked. Also `RLS-AUDIT.md`, `SCHEMA-DRIFT.md`, and the comments in `rls-audit.ts`, `rls-live.ts`, `developer.ts` and `pending-migrations.ts`. Archived docs left alone (RULEBOOK §10.5). **⚠️ Consolidating fixes WHERE things live, not WHAT production has** — the three sets were hand-pasted over months in an unrecorded order and that editor cannot be opened. That remains #558's question, and Vida → Engine → **Schema probe** answers six of it live. **1540 across 91 files** (1527/90 before), gate 6/6 green twice. No writes to production; no behaviour change.
- **30 Jul (🔎 #558's CHECKLIST POINTED AT A SCREEN THAT DOES NOT EXIST — six of its eight questions are now a button):** the doc I shipped an hour earlier ended with eight read-only queries and the instruction *"paste these into Vida → Engine → SQL"*. **There is no SQL runner.** The only SQL path in the product is `POST /operator/migrations/run`, which executes the reviewed `PENDING_MIGRATIONS` constants and deliberately refuses anything else — correct, and not something to widen — and `DATABASE_URL` is mangled, so there is no Postgres connection either. **Eight correct queries with nowhere to run them is a finding that sits there**, which is the failure mode #558 is *about*. The founder caught it by asking the only question that would surface it: *"what must i do in Vida"*. **The fix is small because the questions did not actually need SQL.** PostgREST cannot read `information_schema` — but it does not have to: **selecting a column that does not exist is an error with a specific code** (`42703`/`PGRST204`; `42P01`/`PGRST205` for a table), and selecting one that does is a clean empty result. **So the request IS the probe.** `lib/schema-probe.ts` + `GET /operator/schema-probe` + a Vida → Engine card answer six questions with the supabase-js client we already have, every call a `select … limit 0` head count: nothing written, no row read, no policy in the way. **⚠️ THE RULE THE WHOLE THING TURNS ON, and it is the reason the classifier is pure: a probe that could not RUN returns ❓ UNKNOWABLE, never "missing".** A bad key, a paused project, a dropped connection and an RLS refusal all arrive as errors. Reading any of them as *"the column is absent"* turns an outage into a **confident, wrong schema verdict** — and sends somebody to add a column that was there all along. That is #565's fourth appearance this week, and it is the branch that **cannot be tested against a working database**, which is exactly why the judgement lives in a pure function with no db import. Unknowable renders **RED**, not grey: a state we could not determine never wears the colour of one we did. **The Run-migrations question is answered directly rather than described:** the probe head-counts `hold`/`release` and `migrationSafety()` **refuses to say "safe" on an unmeasured count** — an unread count is not zero, the sending panel's rule (#576) applied to a different number. LAUNCH-PAD's *"run this migration"* action now says press the probe first. **No input, deliberately.** The probe list is a constant and the route reads no `query`/`body`/`params`, so it cannot become the arbitrary-read surface `pending-migrations.ts` refuses to be — asserted by test, and red-proved by adding a `req.query.table`. **And the two questions it CANNOT answer travel with the six it can**, because discovering later that a page never covered something is how it gets trusted for what it does not say: the live `pg_constraint` definition (#558's literal example) and the full `information_schema` census both need a real Postgres connection. **Both are blocked on `DATABASE_URL`, and that one Railway paste also unblocks Run migrations, the RLS audit and the backup manifest — the highest-leverage environment fix outstanding.** **⚠️ AND A STALE ASSERTION IN #558'S OWN TEST WAS REWRITTEN RATHER THAN DELETED, because what it reveals is the lesson:** it asserted the doc *contained* specific SQL, and it **passed the entire time that SQL had nowhere to run.** A test bound to the *presence of a query* cannot tell whether the query is *reachable* — the same shape as a check that passes without running (#581) and a comment-bound assertion that cannot fail for the reason it names. It is now bound to the ACTION. 23 new tests, red-proved five ways. **1527 across 90 files** (1503/89 before), gate 6/6 green twice. Still no writes to production; #273 still not started. ⚠️ `apps/admin` is the operator console — live on merge.
- **30 Jul (🧬 #558 — THE MIGRATIONS DO NOT DESCRIBE PRODUCTION, AND THE REASON IS WORSE THAN THE EXAMPLE):** the item cites one symptom (the live `credit_transactions` type CHECK). Walking the repo found the cause. **126 migration files across THREE directories, and exactly ONE runner that applies TWELVE of them.** There is no `supabase/config.toml`, so the Supabase CLI was never wired up; `PENDING_MIGRATIONS` — a TypeScript constant executed from Vida → Engine — is the only mechanism the product has ever had. **The other 114 were pasted into the Supabase SQL editor by hand**, in an unrecorded order, at unrecorded times, with no record of which ones took — **and that editor can no longer be opened.** So the honest statement of #558 is not *"a column is wrong"*, it is **"nothing in the repo knows what ran."** Three files each claim to be *the* schema (10 tables · 54 · 13) while the migrations create 68. **FIXED, and provable without touching production:** `packages/db/src/schema.sql` was missing **76 columns its own migrations add** to the four tables it declares — `clients.wallet_balance_usd`, `clients.is_demo`, `clients.sourcing_allowance`, `clients.plan`, `leads.delivered_at`, `leads.revealed_at`, `leads.consent_token`, `leads.surfaced_for_approval_at` among them. **That is the money column, the demo flag and every timestamp the approve → surface → charge loop turns on: anyone who ran that file to stand up a database got one the product could not use.** Reconciled with an idempotent `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` block, each column carrying the migration it came from, nothing dropped. **FIVE ❓ UNKNOWABLE rows — columns the code writes that NOTHING in the repo declares — and the worst one is mine, from yesterday.** `leads.source` is declared by no migration and no snapshot, and **#599's CSV import writes it.** I took that field from `routes/icps.ts:513`, which does write `source` — **to `lead_pool`, a different table.** A column read off the wrong table and carried straight into a new write path, and this sweep is the only thing that caught it. Its other writer (`lib/vida.ts:187`, live for weeks) destructures `{ data }` with no error check, so if the column is absent that path has been failing silently the entire time. Also **`whatsapp_messages` and `subscribers` — two tables no migration creates at all** — and `opt_out_blocklist.whatsapp_number`, on the suppression table **every send passes through**, which demonstrably works in production and which no migration in this repo ever created. **The item's own example, traced:** four migrations redefine `credit_transactions_type_check` and disagree. **Only the last one has a runner, and it is the one that DROPS `hold`/`release`** — which `20260724_one_wallet.sql` deliberately kept *"so old rows validate"*. `ADD CONSTRAINT` **validates existing rows**, so if production holds a single `type='hold'` row, **pressing Run migrations throws** — and LAUNCH-PAD had *"Run migration 20260726_wallet_tx_types"* sitting on it as an unqualified action. That row now carries the warning and the query that answers it first. **⚠️ TWO BUGS IN MY OWN INSTRUMENT, and they are the reason to write this down rather than patch quietly.** The scanner reported a table called `table` (from `schema-drift.ts`'s own doc comment) and a column called `compat` — which exists **only in the words "Back-compat" in a comment on `figsy.ts:1691`**. The second was the real one: the shared comment-stripper handled quotes and regex literals but **not nested template literals**, and `figsy.ts:232` holds a backtick inside a `${…}` inside a backtick. The outer template ended at the inner one and **stripping stopped for the remaining 2,700 lines of the file.** The harmless half of that bug invents a finding; **the dangerous half hides a real one** — and fixing it is exactly what surfaced `subscribers.source`. #561's environment sweep uses the same stripper; re-run, and unchanged at 100. Third instrument bug this week, after #581 and my own env scanner. **AND THE SCAN NOW STATES ITS OWN BLIND SPOT, which red-proving forced:** breaking the CSV import by adding a bogus column **did not fail the suite**, because the route inserts a VARIABLE (`insert(accepted)`) and the columns live in `toLeadRow`. It reads **296 of 346** write calls — inline object literals only — and `leads` alone has 12 indirect ones. So ✅ means *"nothing undeclared among the writes I can read"*, never *"nothing undeclared"*, and that sentence is now in the code, the doc and a test. **8 read-only queries on the founder checklist**, copy-pasteable into Vida → Engine, with a test asserting every fenced query on the page is a `SELECT`. **No writes to production, and #273 deliberately not started** — `schema.sql` still declares 10 of 68 tables, which is that item's job, not this one's. 23 tests, red-proved six ways. **1503 across 89 files** (1480/88 before), gate 6/6 green twice. Docs + one schema file; no behaviour change.
- **30 Jul (⚙️ THE ENVIRONMENT, DOCUMENTED — and the number was wrong twice, the second time by my own hand):** #561 recorded **69 variables**; the real figure is **100** across the three deployed apps (**83** read by the API, **17** by the portal/admin only). **The first error was method.** A `process.env.X` grep cannot see the **12 variables this repo reaches by indirection** — `process.env[bundle.priceEnvVar]` (`stripe.ts` builds six price-ID names out of `PRICING`), `has('NAME')` (`routes/engine.ts`'s probe, and the *only* reader of `PAYSTACK_SECRET_KEY`), and `{ key: 'NAME' }` (`startup-check.ts`'s own table, the only place three `STRIPE_PRICE_LEADGEN_*` names appear **at all**). The old sweep counted the shape it expected rather than the reads that exist. **⚠️ THE SECOND ERROR WAS MINE, AND IT IS THE SAME DEFECT THIS REPO KEEPS FINDING IN ITS OWN INSTRUMENTS.** My first scanner stripped comments quote-aware but **not regex-aware**, so a literal like `/key:\s*['"]([A-Z]+)['"]/g` — quotes inside a regex — desynchronised its quote tracking, and from that point it went **blind to comments for the rest of the file**. It surfaced because the sweep returned a variable named **`FOO` that exists only in a sentence in a comment**: a measuring instrument reporting something that was never there. The dangerous half is the inverse — the same desync hides a **genuinely new read** in the noise it invents. Fixed, and the count went 96 → 100. Same family as #581 (a check that passed without running) and #578/#579 (instruments that could not run on the founder's Mac): **the tool that measures is the tool least likely to be measured.** **`startup-check.ts` is the RUNTIME half of the doc and it covered 22 of 83** — reporting on a quarter of the environment and staying silent about the rest, which at boot reads exactly like an environment with nothing else in it. All 83 now carry a tier. **Two new tiers, and the second is the point:** `platform` (Railway/Node set it — never reported missing, because a boot block that nags about `PORT` every deploy is a block nobody reads) and **`parked`, where PRESENCE is the fault, not absence.** The check could previously only complain about a MISSING value, and **the most dangerous variable in this repo is dangerous when SET** — `HOUSE_CLIENT_ID` un-parks the Instantly push that #593 parked yesterday. Five are parked; the boot log now names any that are set. **Three real faults fell out of doing it:** ① **`INBOX_SECRET_KEY` was missing from the go-live SENDING capability**, so that block could print **✅ CLIENT SENDING while not one stored mailbox password could be decrypted** — a false green on the one line whose entire job is to prevent false greens; ② **`SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` was effectively CRITICAL and listed nowhere** — `middleware/auth.ts` calls `createClient(url!, process.env.SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!)` at module scope and supabase-js **throws** on an undefined key, so the API does not boot without it; ③ **`DEPLOYMENT_GUIDE.md` held a partial copy of the same fact that had gone WRONG** — ~20 of 100, and it instructed you to set three `NEXT_PUBLIC_STRIPE_PRICE_LEADGEN_*` variables **nothing in the codebase reads**. A short list is survivable; a short list containing fictional entries teaches you the doc is approximate. Replaced with a pointer (RULEBOOK §10.1, one owner per fact) and the DOC-MAP row moved with it. **One existing guard had to be TIGHTENED rather than satisfied:** #352's `paystack-removed.test.ts` asserted `PAYSTACK_SECRET_KEY` appears nowhere in `startup-check.ts`, and the sweep now lists it there as **parked, with a description that says delete it from Railway**. Its real intent was never *"the string must not appear"* — it was *"nothing may tell the founder to configure a payment path that was removed"*, so it now asserts **the tier itself**. That is strictly stronger: the old version would have passed on `{ key: 'PAYSTACK_SECRET_KEY_V2', level: 'critical' }`. **The doc cannot go stale silently:** `env-doc-drift.test.ts` sweeps all three trees and fails if any variable lacks a **table row** — and that binding was itself found by red-proving, because the first version accepted a name **mentioned in a sentence**, which carries no tier, no what-breaks and no service. It fails in reverse too, so the doc cannot invent a variable. **⚠️ It deliberately does NOT bolt the 17 portal/admin variables onto the API's startup check:** they live in other Railway services, so checking them there would report all 17 missing on a perfectly healthy deploy. The gap is documented, not faked. 18 tests, red-proved five ways. **1480 across 88 files** (1461/87 before), gate 6/6 green twice. Docs + boot-log only; no client-facing surface.
- **30 Jul (🔑 CLIENT ZERO, SET UP ENTIRELY THROUGH VIDA — because there is no SQL editor to fall back on):** tomorrow the founder buys four Google mailboxes, and the Supabase dashboard is unreachable on this account (the same flag that keeps GitHub Actions dead), so **"run this INSERT" is not a step anybody can take.** Every part of getting our own outreach live had to be a control. **Most of it already existed, and I verified that by reading it rather than assuming:** the SMTP columns, the AES-256-GCM password scheme, `pickSendingInbox`, and `sendReadiness` — which already answers *"can this client send?"* **through the same call the send path makes**. None of it was re-implemented. Three gaps were real. **⚠️ THE FIRST ONE IS THE KIND YOU ONLY FIND BY TRYING TO USE THE PAGE: there was no way to add a SECOND mailbox.** The only control that created an inbox row was *Assign pooled inbox*, and it renders **inside the "Cannot send" card** — a list built by filtering for clients that *cannot send*. So the instant mailbox #1 saved with working credentials, the client left that list and **the button vanished with three mailboxes still to add.** A control that removes itself the moment it half-succeeds; the same shape as the migration card that hid once the first migration ran, and it would have stopped tomorrow dead. It also could never set `provider`, `daily_cap` or a status of the operator's choosing, because `/inboxes/assign` hardcodes `active` and `/inboxes/brand` hardcodes `warming` — **which endpoint you called decided the state**, which is backwards. **②** `clients.user_id` is NOT NULL and unique, so a house client row cannot be conjured — it needs an auth user, and the founder has one. **ADOPT BEATS CREATE, and that is the whole design:** signing into the portal already creates a client row, so minting a second leaves two accounts for one person with nothing deciding which is real — **#584 happening again, on purpose this time.** Two house rows is a **refusal**, not a coin toss. No auth user is **also** a refusal: we could call `auth.admin.createUser` (the demo seeder does), but that means generating a password for the founder's own identity, and asking him to sign in once is the smaller ask. Comped with **`manual_grant`** — the existing pattern, already inside `PAID_TX_TYPES` *because* "a manual grant is how we comp a client" — rather than inventing a house-only flag; without it the sourcing money-gate refuses and Client Zero has four mailboxes and an empty desk. **Idempotent**, and pressing it twice writes nothing. **③ Readiness was only ever shown for FAILURE.** A client that COULD send appeared nowhere on the page at all, so *"everything is fine"* and *"missing for some other reason"* rendered identically — **absence read as health, which is #565 wearing yet another hat.** Every client now carries a verdict either way, and the colour draws a line that matters: **a missing `INBOX_SECRET_KEY` is RED because it kills EVERY client, not this one**, and an **unknown** state (`lookup_failed`/`check_failed`) is RED too, never the same amber as an ordinary not-set-up-yet. `needs_inbox` is now DERIVED from that single list, so the two cannot drift apart. **Password discipline, and one assertion I nearly left out:** never stored, returned or audited in plaintext — the API returns neither the password nor the ciphertext, the audit records only `password_set: true`. But *"the stored value is not the password"* would pass on any mangled string, so it also **round-trips through `decryptSecret`** — otherwise the test greens while nothing can ever send. **Key unset = REFUSED, and no row written**: the tempting wrong answer is *"save the mailbox and add the password later"*, which leaves a credential-less row reading as ADDED on the board (#552 exactly). **Warm-up defaults to 21 days rather than the client SOP's 14**, deliberately: that route records a mailbox a vendor pre-warmed, while these are boxes bought this morning with no reputation and the founder's own plan says 3–4 weeks — quoting 14 would put a "ready" date on screen a week before it is true. It is a **reminder, not permission**; **#553's ladder** decides. **⚠️ `HOUSE_CLIENT_ID` STAYS UNSET**, and the warning renders beside the id every single time — because the id is *precisely* what makes that variable look like the last step of setup. It gates the **parked** Instantly push (#593) and nothing about our own sending. 47 tests (24 pure + 23 route), red-proved five ways — plaintext storage, the swallowed key check, create-instead-of-adopt, a swallowed client-list read (which would mint a SECOND house account beside the real one), and an unknown state painted amber. **1461 across 87 files** (1414/85 before), gate 6/6 green twice. ⚠️ `apps/admin` is the operator console — live on merge.
- **30 Jul (📥 THE DOOR IN — Client Zero's list can finally reach the product it was built for):** our engine can only mail what is in our tables, and **there was no way to put a list into them.** The one inbound lead path, `POST /figsy/webhook/enrol` (#250), is the wrong shape in every dimension: **per-CLIENT developer-key auth** (we are the operator, not the client), **straight into a campaign** (skipping the desk entirely), and it **CHARGES on the way in** — `chargeFigsyEnroll` runs charge-first (#310/#332), so pushing a thousand Apollo prospects through it would have **billed for a thousand leads nobody approved.** That is not a small misuse; it is the money model backwards, since **approve is the only money event.** So `POST /operator/import-leads` is operator-authed and **touches no money at all** — asserted by importing 100 people in a test and proving the ledger stayed empty. **The gates are REUSED, not reimplemented,** and that was the whole design decision: `routes/icps.ts` pool-serve already drops the opt-out blocklist, the do-not-contact floor and anything the client already owns, so a second copy of that sequence **is** how an import path ends up mailing someone who opted out. The judgement moved into `lib/lead-import.ts` — pure, no database — and the route only fetches the two sets and writes the rows. Imported leads land `status:'pending'`, `delivered_at:null`, `source:'csv_import'`, `icp_id:null`, **identical to a sourced lead**, so they appear on the desk and flow approve → enrol → send with nothing downstream knowing where they came from. **⚠️ THE #349 SHAPE, and this time it was in a READ rather than a write:** `owned` and `blocked` come back from supabase-js as `{ data, error }`, and an **unchecked read yields an empty set that is indistinguishable from *"nobody is blocked"*.** The failure is silent, plausible and maximally expensive — the import proceeds and mails exactly the people the gate exists to catch. Both reads now **abort the entire import** and say which one failed and that nothing was written. **Every skipped row is named, with its spreadsheet line number and a reason** — imported / duplicate / suppressed / invalid — because a partial import reporting only a success count is #349 in file form: 1,000 uploaded, *"imported"* shown, 300 silently gone. And a **mid-write failure reports the TRUE count** (*"250 of 600 were saved"*) rather than a total, because re-uploading is the right next move only if the number is honest — which it is, since the duplicate gate makes a re-run safe. **A demo client is refused with the reason**: real people in a demo account get mailed the next time it is shown to a prospect. **The parser is hand-rolled and RFC-4180-correct rather than a dependency or a `split(',')`** — `"Acme, Inc."` in the company column would shift every field after it one place left and import job titles into the country field, a corruption that still *looks* like a lead. Column names are matched across alias lists because Apollo's export headers are not stable and the founder may hand-edit the file; guessing one header costs the entire import. **A dry run is the primary button** — for a thousand rows, *"look at what would happen"* and *"hope"* are different operations — and the **1,000-row cap is stated on screen before a file is chosen**, since a limit you discover on rejection is a trap. **Not a contradiction of #549's *"no CSV export"* lock:** that forbids exporting OUT of our product into a vendor's tool; this is a list coming IN so that **our own product is what runs the outreach**, which is the same decision pointing the same way. 38 tests (22 pure + 16 route), red-proved four ways — naive comma splitting, dropped within-file dedupe, a delivered-at that would have bypassed the desk, and both swallowed reads. **1414 across 85 files** (1376/83 before), gate 6/6 green twice. ⚠️ `apps/admin` is the operator console — live on merge.
- **30 Jul (📤 the glance — `/vida/sending`, and the number it refuses to invent):** Client Zero sends through our own engine now, so the founder's condition was *"we need a way to monitor the break."* Alerts are the push; this is the pull. **A new page rather than an extension of the System screen, and the reason matters:** #576 runs **on a button** and probes Stripe/Resend/Anthropic — slow on purpose, right for *"is everything wired"*. *"Is outreach working right now"* has to be glanceable. **A probe and a glance are different tools**, and the alternative home (`vida/page.tsx`) is 2,000 lines of worklist. **⚠️ THE FINDING THAT SHAPED THE WHOLE PANEL, and it was only visible by reading the write path rather than the schema: FAILED SENDS ARE NOT RECORDED ANYWHERE.** `sendSequenceEmail` inserts the `figsy_sent_emails` row **before** the send (it needs the id for the tracking pixel) and **DELETES it when the send fails**, rolls the enrollment back to retry, and alerts by email. **No row survives, and no outcome event is written either** — so that table is a record of **successes only**, and *"failed: 0"* would have been a confident claim about something never counted. It renders **NOT MEASURED with the reason and the fix** (a status column instead of the delete, or a `send_failures` row), left as its own change rather than smuggled into a read-only panel. **That is the same discipline as the System screen's NOT-MEASURED state, applied to a number I could easily have faked and nobody would have noticed until a send broke silently.** **Two judgement rules, pure and unit-tested:** ① **zero sent is AMBER when sending is expected** — kill-switch on, active campaign, enrollments due — and calm otherwise, because a dead cron and a quiet Sunday rendering identically is #565 wearing a different hat; ② **bounces are judged as a RATE**: 5 bounces reads RED on 20 sends and OK on 2,000, since the raw count either cries wolf at scale or stays silent while a small mailbox dies. The bounce/opt-out split reuses `opt_out_blocklist`'s own reason strings and **pins them by test against what `routes/figsy.ts` actually upserts**, so a renamed reason fails loudly instead of silently reporting zero bounces. Both are **house-wide, not per client** — said on screen, because the blocklist is global by design. 20 tests, red proven (3 fail). **1376 across 83 files** (1356/82 before), gate 6/6 green. ⚠️ `apps/admin` is the operator console — live on merge.
- **30 Jul (🏗️ CLIENT ZERO RUNS ON OUR OWN ENGINE — the founder amended his own #577 lock, walked point by point):** the question was *"do we need Instantly at all?"*, and it only resolved by splitting the bundle into its seven parts and asking each separately. **Of the seven we lacked exactly ONE: the warmup network.** Mailboxes are bought separately either way; the **sequencer is FIGSY**, the **sender is #547/#548**, the **unibox is ours and smarter** (AI classification, hot alerts, CRM push) — all three duplicate *the product we are trying to prove*. And the punchline: **the $97 HyperGrowth tier was needed ONLY for the API, and the API existed only to integrate with a sender we no longer use.** We were about to pay a premium for the privilege of leaving our own engine idle. **NEW LOCK: our engine sends our outreach · Instantly is a warmup utility on Growth (~$37, vendor-confirmed campaign-free) · Smartlead = clients, unchanged.** **⚠️ THE CONSTRAINT THAT MAKES THE PURCHASE SPECIFIC, and it was already in #577 waiting to be read:** both vendors confirmed in writing that their mailboxes **expose no SMTP credentials**, and `sending-inbox.ts:63` requires `smtp_host && smtp_user && smtp_pass_enc`. **So the boxes must be bought DIRECT from Google (~$6) — Instantly's done-for-you box is $1 CHEAPER and cannot be sent through by our engine at all.** A cheaper option that silently breaks the architecture is exactly the kind of thing that gets bought by accident, so it is now a test. **Nothing deleted (rule 3):** `instantly.ts`/`-map`/`-push` are **parked** as the revival path if our deliverability fails the #553 ladder — and the step-9 push needed **no new switch**, because `HOUSE_CLIENT_ID` being unset already means nothing is ever pushed; a fail-closed safety property doing double duty as the park. **The planned Instantly reply feeder is dead and that is a win** — replies to our own mailboxes land in our own pipeline, so the provider-agnostic spine (#589) proved itself in the other direction too: a provider we decided *not* to use cost nothing to drop. **Money, three layers:** floor **~$283 → ~$223/mo** · outreach rent ~$62 (boxes $25 + Growth $37) · **fuel ~$127–137 per 1,000 prospects** (Apollo + ~$8 verification + ~$70 AI writing; sending is $0 because it is ours) ≈ **one client** conservatively, ~$85/mo while hunting. **Apollo for us, PDL+Hunter for clients** — the same mirror as Instantly/Smartlead, and it matters: the identical 1,000 names on PDL would be $280, turning ~$130 of fuel into ~$384. **And the guard caught me deleting something:** rewriting the LAUNCH-PAD purchase order silently removed the three founder money-checks added an hour earlier — `cost-floor-drift.test.ts` failed on the teardown-order assertion and the block was restored. **A test written to protect a document from drift protected it from its own author.** Guard also **inverted** (it demanded 'HyperGrowth'; now demands 'Growth' + the reason) and extended with the SMTP-credentials constraint. 18 tests, red proven. **1356 across 82 files**, gate 6/6 green twice.
- **30 Jul (💷 IDLE TOOLS BILL NOTHING — the cost register now says what we actually spend, not what the model spends):** the founder is funding this out of pocket, and the register was quoting **client-scale** figures for a business with **zero clients**. Split into **NOW** and **FUTURE**, with the rule written down rather than left as bare zeroes — *a $0 line with no trigger reads as "this tool is free"*, which is the opposite of true and is **exactly how the old ~$190 floor came to omit four paid tools**. Every $0 line now carries its switch-back-on trigger: **Hunter** and **PDL** the month a sourcing run happens · **Smartlead** the day a client signs · the **failover** when there are clients to feel an outage. **Floor ~$423 → ~$283/mo**, and **~$550** at the first client. Hunter 110→0 (it verifies during a run and does nothing between them) · Anthropic 45→10 (actual at our-own-volume) · Domains 10→3 and the registrar **named as GoDaddy for the first time**. **The failover is being cancelled and that is the one line with a blast radius:** $7 Render `kind-api-standby` + $5 Cloudflare Load Balancer, insurance on an empty house — but `api.get-kind.com` routes **through** that load balancer, so **deleting it before repointing DNS to Railway takes the live product down.** The order is now written into the model, the register and the founder's to-do list, and a test asserts the three steps stay in that order. **`render.yaml` and the runbook stay in the repo** — cancelling a service is not deleting the recipe (rule 3), and they are what brings it back the day an outage costs revenue. **Claude Code corrected to the real invoice, £119.99 ≈ $152** (it was a ~$150 guess), and **moved outside the platform floor**: it is the founder's **build tool, not product infrastructure** — the product does not need it to run for a client, only to be built. All-in out of pocket **~$435/mo**. Instantly's DFY mailbox pricing confirmed and recorded (Google ×4 + 2 domains ≈ $23/mo, **bought through Instantly rather than GoDaddy**, so they sit where the mailboxes are). Drift guard 10 → **17 tests**, red proven by deleting the idle rule, dropping the DNS-first warning and reordering the teardown. **1355 across 82 files** (1348/82 before), gate 6/6 green twice. ⚠️ **Three founder checks worth up to ~$220/mo are now on LAUNCH-PAD** — Hunter/PDL idle billing, the ordered failover teardown, and a GoDaddy domain audit; the docs record the decisions, the checks confirm reality.
- **30 Jul (💸 INSTANTLY-FIRST — and the tier we were about to buy was the wrong one, on the vendor's own bad advice):** founder reversed the purchase order. **Smartlead is deferred** (client-sending infrastructure buys nothing until a client exists) and **Instantly HyperGrowth goes first** — it runs OUR outreach, so it is the line item that goes and *finds* the first client, and at ~$121/mo contribution per client it pays for itself at roughly one. **The catch, and it nearly cost real money:** `lib/instantly.ts` said API v2 needs *"the Growth plan or above"* — **text lifted from Instantly's own 402 error body.** Their published plan-comparison table lists **API: No** and **Webhooks: No** on Growth. So the vendor's error string is wrong about the vendor's own product, and following it would have bought a **$97/mo tier on which none of the code we already wrote can run** — no `/accounts`, no `/campaigns`, no `/leads` push, and no webhook for the reply feeder. **HyperGrowth is the minimum.** The generalisable lesson: **a comment or a test that encodes what a third party TOLD us is only as good as the third party**, and the fix is to read their pricing table, not their error string. **An existing test had pinned the wrong name**, and correcting it exposed a second assertion **passing for the wrong reason** — `'hypergrowth plan'` *contains* the substring `'growth plan'`, so `toContain('growth plan')` survived the change silently. Same family as binding a test to a comment: it could not fail for the reason it named. Both now assert the tier. **The floor moved DOWN, which is the counter-intuitive part:** −$94 Smartlead, −$37 the old Instantly guess, +$97 the real tier = **~$457 → ~$423/mo**, returning to ~$517 the day a client signs. Mirrored across `CASHFLOW-LAB.html` (Smartlead $0 with the reason, Instantly $97, a new editable line for sending domains/mailboxes), `run-costs-and-cashflow.md`, the LAUNCH-PAD purchase order and the #556 figure. **And a new drift guard, because this number has now drifted five times** ($138 → $203 → ~$175–190 → ~$457 → ~$423): 10 tests assert the three documents AGREE, that the computed floor sums **every row the page renders** — a row present in the HTML but missing from `FIXED_IDS` is editable, visible, and silently excluded, which reads as accounted for — and that the historical figures stay marked as history. **The first version of that guard was itself too weak** (`toContain` over the whole file passed when the headline said $999, because the right number appeared further down in the correction history); it is now bound to the specific claim sites. **1348 across 82 files** (1338/81 before), gate 6/6 green twice after one genuine red. Docs + comments; no behaviour change.
- **30 Jul (⚖️ #413 + #327 — two items filed as "already fixed, just verify", and neither was; one of them was in the CONTRACT):** both rows described defects that no longer existed, which is worse than an open item — anyone verifying found the old wording gone and moved on. **#413's real defect, found by reading the pages against the code: after #562 the first $99 does NOT load the wallet, it buys the onboarding pack, and the first 100 approvals are INCLUDED. The public site said the opposite in SIX places, including the terms, which is a contract.** `terms.html` (*"$99, which loads your wallet"*, plus §5 and §6 both describing the flat $4 as if it were the only behaviour) and `pricing.html` (hero, wallet strip, FAQ, and **two meta descriptions** — the ones that show up in search results and link previews). **It under-promises rather than over-charges, so no client was ever billed wrongly** — but a contract that misdescribes what money buys is its own problem, and *"100 leads included for $99"* is a materially better offer than *"$99 loads your wallet, then $4 each"*. **We were underselling the thing while also misstating it.** Exactly the defect fixed on the billing card in #1226, one surface over — which is the argument for fixing a claim everywhere it appears rather than where it was reported. §5's *"never consume a credit"* list now names the pack approvals, because that list is the one place in the contract a client actually looks. **#327's survivor turned out to be a different claim and half true:** `support.html` said a client *"can export your approved leads as a CSV"* — the **endpoint exists** (`/leads/export/csv`, authed, live) but its only UI is in the retired `(dashboard)` tree that middleware redirects clients out of, so **there is no button a client can reach.** Corrected to *"email us and we'll send it — there is no self-serve export yet"*, and the **CRM-dedupe half was KEPT because it is true**; replacing a false claim with a lost one is not an improvement. 18 tests read the real HTML with every figure checked against `PACK_LEADS`/`PACK_PRICE_USD`/`LEAD_PRICE_USD`, red proven (11 fail). Rider ① (#570's dot) was **already satisfied** — #1227 merged and flipped it. **1338 across 81 files** (1320/80 before), gate 6/6 green. ⚠️ **Contractual + client-facing — preview first, and #432's lawyer pass should cover this wording.**
- **30 Jul (📮 #547 DECIDED — consent email stays on the transactional domain, and PR #1221 is CLOSED as outdated):** the open sub-question was *which of OUR domains* a POPIA consent request leaves from. #1221 moved it to `FIGSY_COLD_FROM`, and that was correct reasoning against a stack we no longer have: **the founder's locked model is Instantly = US, Smartlead = CLIENTS, Zoho `hello@get-kind.com` = WEBSITE INBOUND — there is no separately-warmed cold domain in it.** With `FIGSY_COLD_FROM` unset, `COLD_FROM` already falls back to the transactional identity, so #1221 was a **runtime no-op** that would only have added a log warning. **Decision: consent stays on `hello@get-kind.com` for now; #1221 is closed, not merged; consent's long-term home is the INSTANTLY lane** — *us* emailing strangers is exactly what Instantly is for — **decided when #549 unblocks (blocked on the Instantly bill, API 402).** Two things stay true meanwhile and are worth keeping in view: a stranger's reply or complaint lands in the Zoho inbox that is meant for website enquiries, and the complaint hits the address every invoice and password reset also leaves from. **Live risk today is nil** — auto-consent at sourcing is gated behind `AUTO_OUTREACH_ENABLED` (off), and the manual consent button lives in the retired `(dashboard)` tree that middleware redirects clients out of. This needs revisiting **before** #553's first-send ladder, not before the next PR.
- **29 Jul (🪟 #570③ — the item was wrong about its own defect, and the comment was the bug):** ③ is filed as a **silent** mismatch: *"the KPI can read 200 beside a panel showing 50, with no load more"*. **It is not silent.** `deskCoverage()` renders *"Showing the top 50 of 200. Approve or pass some to see the rest."* directly above the list — wired at `milla/page.tsx:383` and already unit-tested. The 50 are the top 50 **by score**, and working through them surfaces the next batch, which is the #567 model rather than a truncation. **So I did not build the paging the prompt asked for:** it would layer a client-facing feature over a working disclosure, and whether 50 is the right window is a product decision, not a defect — raising the limit being explicitly rejected already (#571: *"a bigger limit is the same bug with a later trigger"*). **What was actually broken was the comment at `leads.ts:310` asserting *"leads_awaiting mirrors /for-approval exactly"* — false, and the reason ③ read as already-fixed for weeks: anyone checking found a comment asserting the very thing they came to verify.** Rewritten to state the real relationship, with 5 guards so the disclosure cannot be silently unwired. **And the block caught the trap it was written about — twice.** First my replacement comment *quotes* the old false claim, so an unstripped `not.toContain` matched the quotation and failed; then stripping comments broke the three assertions that are *about* the comment. The fix is **two views of one file**: stripped code for "no longer claims X", raw text for "the file documents X". Fourth comment-binding trap this week and **the first that failed loudly rather than passing falsely** — which is the whole argument for the practice. Dot reconciled **🔴→🟡**: the row text has said *"stays 🟡"* since PR #1223 but the flip never ran, and **doc-lint does not catch prose-vs-dot drift** — worth knowing, since the board is the thing we have been trusting. **STILL OPEN, founder call:** is 50 the right window, or should the panel page? 18 tests (was 13), red proven (4 fail). **1313 across 80 files**, gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (💳 #563 — the card a client reads before entering their card number said two false things, and the reason was structural):** the `$99` starter card read *"Fund your wallet. Each approved lead is a flat $4."* Since #562 **neither half is true**: the first payment does not credit the wallet (`isPackPurchase` skips `increment_wallet` — the $99 buys the pack), and the first 100 approvals are not $4, they are included. **The root cause was not carelessness.** `PACK_LEADS` / `PACK_PRICE_USD` / `LEAD_PRICE_USD` lived only in `apps/api/src/lib/onboarding-pack.ts`, and **the portal cannot import from `apps/api`** — it depends on `@kind/shared` and nothing else. So *every* client-facing sentence about money was **hand-typed**, and a hand-typed price is a lie waiting for the model to change. Moved the three into `@kind/shared` with `onboarding-pack.ts` **re-exporting** them (one source, and `PACK_SOURCE_TARGET` derived as `PACK_LEADS * 2` rather than a second literal), rewrote the card to what is true, and derived every figure. **`packLine` was hand-typing `$4` too** in its exhausted-pack sentence — caught while in the file, now the constant. A guard fails if `WALLET_FIRST_PURCHASE_USD` is ever a literal again. 12 tests (was 5), red proven (4 fail). **1315 across 80 files** (1308/80 before), gate 6/6 green. ⚠️ Client-facing — **preview first**, 🟣 before live.
- **29 Jul (🔚 #568④ — the last swallowed enrol, and the reason it hid longest):** `approve-lead.ts:81`, the **#424 charge-once free re-approve**, still ended in `autoEnrollLead(...).catch(() => {})`. **It hid because it returns `charged: false`, so it reads like a free path.** It is the opposite: `reveal_is_owned` is true *precisely because this client already paid for this contact* — the money moved on an earlier approval, and what the re-approve buys is the **WORK**. Swallowed, they have paid and the lead enters no sequence, which is the exact *"paid, never worked"* outcome the no-campaign gate at step 3c fails closed to prevent and the paid path at step 8 already alerts on. **With ② guarded and ④ silent, the failure simply moved to whichever door the client came through** — which is the argument for finishing a class rather than fixing the instance you found. **The alert is deliberately NOT the paid path's, and a test pins that the two never converge:** reusing *"They were charged $4"* would send the operator hunting a charge that does not exist on this approval and would contradict the `charged: false` in the response, so this one says **no new charge was made and they had ALREADY paid**. The lead stays approved and still reports `charged: false` — flipping it would recreate #569's defect pointed the other way. **`:69` (demo) stays swallowed BY DECISION and is now commented and pinned** so nobody completes the set out of symmetry: nobody paid, `is_demo` is a hard stop in the send path, every address is `.invalid`, and paging the founder about a seeded walkthrough row is precisely how they learn to ignore the alert that means a real client is stuck. **The harness switch that found this is now deliberate rather than constant** — `reveal_is_owned` was pinned to `false` while only the paid path was guarded, and flipping it is what sent an assertion down this branch to pass while proving nothing; both branches are now driven. **All four #568 sites are closed and pinned, so it flips 🩷** (① `pack-boundary` · ②③④ `paid-lead-delivery`, 7 new tests). Red proven (4 fail on re-swallowing). **1308 across 80 files** (1301/80 before), gate 6/6 green twice. ⚠️ Correct-by-test, not by observation: no client has yet hit a real enrol failure.
- **29 Jul (📥 #551 — the Smartlead reply feeder, and the 256 lines that had to move before it could exist):** the prompt asked for a feeder. Reading `reply-ingest.ts` first, as it instructed, turned up the thing the prompt did not know: the spine made the **judgements** provider-agnostic, but the **256 lines that ACT on them** — store the reply, classify once, suppress opt-outs, escalate risky replies, run the hot path, push to CRM — were still **inside the Resend Express handler**. So a second feeder had exactly two options, duplicate or move, and duplicating is the one thing #589 exists to forbid: *"each of the three providers grows its own copy of the same five bugs."* **R7 alone — a failed blocklist write meaning we keep emailing someone who told us to stop — is not a defect to own two copies of.** Extracted to `lib/reply-pipeline.ts`. **Deliberately a MOVE, not a rewrite:** four mechanical changes and no behavioural ones, and the proof is that **all 59 existing reply tests stayed green**, `reply-fanout.route.test.ts` among them because it drives the real Express handler. Only **two source-scan assertions** broke, both reading `routes/figsy.ts` for calls that had moved — repointed at the pipeline, plus a new one asserting the route **DELEGATES** rather than re-inlining the loop, so this cannot silently regress. `figsy.ts` 3240→2999 lines. **One of those repointed tests then failed for the wrong reason and that is worth recording:** `indexOf(name)` matched the IMPORT at the top of the pipeline, so `slice(i - 600, …)` went **negative** and JavaScript quietly read from the END of the string, giving an empty window. Same family as binding to a comment — **an assertion has to measure the thing it names** — fixed by binding to the call site (`name({`) and clamping with `Math.max(0, …)`. **The feeder:** `POST /figsy/replies/smartlead`, thin by design — authenticate, dedup, parse, delegate. It routes on the **receiving mailbox**, which is the only unambiguous answer to *whose reply is this*: the same prospect replying to client A's mailbox and to client B's lands against A and B respectively, an unknown mailbox **fans out exactly as today** rather than dropping, and a reply at a known mailbox from someone that client does not hold goes to **NEITHER** and alerts — because falling back there is the actual harm, posting one client's inbound mail into another's unibox. SENT/OPEN events are skipped (processing our own outbound as a reply could classify our own email as hot), and dedup is **namespaced by provider** because two providers can legitimately issue the same message id. **Two honest limits, both flagged rather than papered over:** it **ships INERT** — no `SMARTLEAD_WEBHOOK_SECRET` means 503, the right default for a public endpoint that can suppress a client's leads — and **the payload shape is UNVERIFIED** because the key 401s and their docs 403 us, so the field names are inferred, isolated behind a tolerant alias list in ONE function, and an unreadable payload **alerts with the keys Smartlead actually sent** so the fix comes from evidence rather than a second guess. Instantly (#549) deliberately not built — blocked on the bill and untestable. 15 route tests, red proven (6 fail). **1301 across 80 files** (1285/79 before), gate 6/6 green **three consecutive runs** after last session's flake.
- **29 Jul (🪞 the board was the bug — seven items shipped and reading 🔴/🟡, and the two that genuinely weren't):** the founder has spent weeks being told *"we're at 50%"* while the inventory understated what was built. Nine rows audited by **reading the fix bodies on main**, not by trusting the row or a marker string. **Seven were shipped and mis-dotted** — #562 #563 #565 #566 #567 #569 #491, now 🩷. **Four of those had no test at all**, and that is the real deliverable here rather than the dots: a correct money path with nothing pinning it is one refactor from being wrong again. **#562 is the sharpest case** — the $99-paid-twice fix (ledger-derived first-purchase check skipping `increment_wallet`) was live and **completely unpinned**; `pack-double-charge.test.ts` covers `chargeFigsyEnroll`, the *enrol* side, not this webhook's wallet decision, so the single line standing between us and **~$96 given away per client** had nothing holding it. Nine webhook tests now pin both directions, including the `.neq('reference', session.id)` exclusion — load-bearing, because the ledger row for THIS payment would otherwise make a genuine first purchase look like a second and reintroduce the bug through the back door. #568②③ and #563 pinned too (24 new tests across three files, red proven — **11 fail** against the original bodies). **#565 #566 #567 #569 needed no test: already pinned, dot wrong only.** #491 was verified **wired** into `cron.ts` rather than being a lib nothing calls — the check the prompt asked for, and the answer was yes. **TWO STAYED 🟡, both because the audit found something the item never named.** **#570③ is real:** `/for-approval` carries `.limit(50)` while the `leads_awaiting` count has none, so 200 awaiting shows a KPI of 200 beside a panel of 50 — **and the comment above it asserts *"leads_awaiting mirrors /for-approval exactly"*, which is false, and is precisely why the mismatch read as fixed.** Left open: paging the panel is client-facing (preview required) and raising the limit is the same bug with a later trigger, the reasoning #571 already rejected. **#568 has a FOURTH swallow the item never listed** — `approve-lead.ts:81`, the #424 charge-once free re-approve, still `.catch(() => {})` on the enrol. Found only because a harness returning `reveal_is_owned: true` sent an assertion down that branch, where it passed while proving nothing; the client already paid for that contact, so it is the same *"paid, never worked"* outcome through the third of three enrol call sites in one file (`:69`, demo, is deliberately fine). **And a NEW client-facing money defect fell out of #562's own fix:** `billing/page.tsx:361` still says *"Fund your wallet. Each approved lead is a flat $4"* on the **$99 starter card** — after #562 that $99 does not fund the wallet and the first 100 are not $4, so both halves are false on the screen a client reads immediately before paying. Logged on #563, not fixed here (own PR + preview). **And the new tests flaked, which is its own lesson:** the suite went red 1 run in 3 on my own `toHaveLength(0)` over the alert array. Not the product — `subscription-write-errors.test.ts` also drives the stripe router, and when vitest schedules both files into one worker the module graph is shared, so the assertion was counting the OTHER file's alerts. Caught only by running the gate repeatedly instead of accepting the first green; **a 1-in-3 flake reported as green is how a red gate becomes a habit.** Fixed by filtering alerts by subject rather than asserting an empty array — the claim that actually matters, and one no neighbouring file can pollute. Six consecutive clean runs before commit. **Board moves 🩷207→214 · 🟡60→56 · 🔴211→208.** **1285 across 79 files** (1261/76 before), gate 6/6 green.

After the 22-Jul pivot **Milla IS the client portal** and **Vida IS our operator console** — the two things a client logs into and the founder runs the business from, neither of them a subscription anybody can buy. Marked **LEGACY, not deleted** (CORE-MAP rule 3), with a header saying what the names are now and — the half that actually carries risk — that the Stripe Price objects behind them are **DORMANT, not gone**: `/stripe/subscribe` and the subscription webhook still work end to end, so a live Price ID in the dashboard could still take a payment for a product we do not sell. It is unreachable only because `middleware.ts` redirects every signed-in client out of `(dashboard)` to `/milla` — stale copy behind a closed door rather than a live overcharge, but not the "nothing renders these" I had asserted. Its import is load-bearing and deliberately kept; the narrower `.description` claim does hold. **And the tests caught me:** my first guard policed the `name` strings for "AI Virtual Assistant", and it failed against my own change — marking a block LEGACY does not rewrite the names inside it, and rewriting them was not available either, because those labels mirror the dormant Stripe products (the same trap the FIGSY block documents). So the guard became the reachable contradiction instead: **every price a reader could copy out of the block must be named in the header as not-sold**. Constants + three dead imports; no client-facing surface.
- **29 Jul (💸 #349 tail — the writes that decide whether a client has access, and the unsubscribe that didn't unsubscribe):** the wallet half of #349 closed on 27 Jul; this is the other half, where the swallowed error costs not a ledger row but an **authorisation decision**. I swept the partner/subscription family exhaustively rather than estimating: **27 write sites, 12 already checked, 15 fixed** — and the sweep corrected my own earlier count of *"25 sites, 16 unchecked"*, because `stripe.ts:578` turned out to be checked already through the `writeStatus` helper. **The three that matter most all point the same way — the row diverges from what Stripe says the client pays, permanently and silently, because Stripe does not resend these events:** `subscription.deleted` left the row `active` so a cancelled client **keeps the product free**; `invoice.payment_succeeded` left it `past_due` so a client who **paid stays locked out** (the worst direction — we have their money and they can't use it); `invoice.payment_failed` left it `active` so someone who stopped paying kept access. **That last one had a second failure stacked on the first, and it is the sharper finding:** the dunning alert stated *"the subscription is now marked past_due"* **unconditionally**, so the single message that could ever have surfaced the bug asserted the opposite of what happened — a self-reporting system reporting on a write it never checked. It now reads back what actually landed. **The money tail:** the partner-commission insert is unrecoverable when swallowed, because the idempotency guard reads the very row that failed to write, so the next invoice finds nothing and never backfills — the partner is simply short a month with no trace anywhere; and `auth.ts` carried **another literal `.then(()=>{},()=>{})`** on the partner-referral attribution, the only row linking a client to the partner who sent them, without which no commission is ever attributable to that partner again. **The client-facing cancel** answered *"cancelled — access continues until end of period"* over a write that hadn't happened: they stop watching for the charge, Stripe keeps charging, churn never counts them. It now refuses rather than lies. **And the unsubscribe, which is the compliance one:** all three writes in `recordUnsubscribe` ran on bare `await`s, so a failed blocklist upsert still answered *"You have been unsubscribed"* and the next send run saw nothing to stop it — somebody used the mechanism the law requires us to honour, we told them it worked, and we kept emailing them, with **no retry possible** because nobody clicks unsubscribe twice on an email they believe they already stopped. The blocklist failure alerts and **names the address**, since the fix is adding them by hand and an alert without the address cannot be closed; the lead-status write deliberately only logs, because alerting on all three trains the founder to ignore all three. Plus the demo/sandbox seeders, demo-extend, the referral-code guarantee, and the trial-expiry one-shot marker — swallowed, that one emails the same client **daily** until someone notices. **Deliberately untouched:** the two `figsy.ts` send-rollback writes, which already alert on the very next line. **25 tests, every fix red proven** (13 fail against the original bodies). **1252 across 76 files** (1227/74 before), gate 6/6 green. API-only — no client-facing surface, no preview needed.

It now asks first — a `confirm()` that **names the client** (an "are you sure?" on the wrong account is the accident) and says plainly that emails go to **REAL prospects** from that client's own mailbox, with a declined confirm returning rather than falling through. On the Asks tab that is a confident statement of fact about what a client is waiting on, made over an endpoint that was down. Both now route into `loadFail` exactly as #565 does, the screen distinguishes could-not-load from genuinely-empty, and a successful reload clears a stale banner rather than leaving red over healthy data. **⚠️ And the same test trap for the THIRD time, which is now a pattern worth naming:** my ordering assertions matched the string inside the **comment that quotes the old code**, so they "proved" the status call came before its own fix. Identical to the `express.static` bound in #560 and the *"surfaced by the reload"* bound in the #564 residual. Fixed by stripping comment lines before comparing positions — the guard `reply-routing.test.ts` has had all along. **A source-scan bound is only as good as the text it measures**, and three times now the text has included a comment about the bug. 12 guards, red proven (10 fail against the original bodies). **All three #564 defects are now built.** **1227 across 74 files** (1215/74 before), gate 6/6 green. - **29 Jul (🔁 #571 — the same defect in THREE places, and the third one made the other two unreachable):** the morning's fix closed `lib/start-work.ts`. A verify pass then found the identical `.eq('is_active', true).maybeSingle()` — no `.limit(1)` — in **`POST /operator/source`**, and a second verify found it again in **`GET /operator/source-preview`**. That last one is the lesson: the preview renders the *"Source N leads?"* card, which is the step BEFORE the button, so a client with two active ICPs got *"no active ICP"* on the card and **the Source button never rendered** — meaning fixing the button alone would have shipped a working door behind a gate that refused to open, for exactly the client the fix was for. **Neither was fixable with `.limit(1)`**, which is the tempting one-character repair: it stops the PostgREST error and keeps the real bug, one of the client's audiences silently never sourced. Both routes now read every active ICP and **import the existing `splitSourceTarget`** rather than re-deriving the arithmetic — three copies of a split rule is three chances to drift, and drift is what put the same defect in three files. `/source` runs sequentially because the runs share one `try_spend_sourcing` allowance, reports per-ICP failures rather than collapsing them, 502s only when every run fails, and writes `icps_active`/`icps_run` to the audit row where the honest answer used to be *"one, whichever the database happened to return"*. **The preview estimates against the same split, so the card predicts the run** — and counts an overlapping person **once** across ICPs, which is the subtle one: two audiences legitimately overlap, and without the dedupe set each claims the same pool, `pool_free` reads double, `pdl_needed` reads zero, and the operator confirms a run that then spends real PDL money. Over-stating the free pool is the direction that costs us. **`operator.ts:373` and `:673` were checked and deliberately left alone** — both already carry `.limit(1)` before `.maybeSingle()`. 22 route-level tests, driven through the real Express handlers because the helper was never wrong and the bug was always in what the route fed it (#541's lesson). **Red proven twice**, the second time in isolation after the first regression masked it: reverting to a single ICP hid the dedupe test behind a case with nothing to overlap, so the dedupe was re-broken on its own and watched to fail with `pool_free` 20 instead of 10. **1215 across 74 files** (1193/73 before), gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (🧯 #544 — the backstop did not cover the one domain the demo actually uses):** `email.ts isRealRecipient` describes itself as *"the last line of defence: any transactional send to a non-deliverable address is dropped before it hits Resend"*, and it filtered `.internal`, `kind-demo.`, `example.`, `.test` and `localhost` — while **missing `.invalid`, the TLD every MBF cast member carries** (`mbf-demo.invalid`). **It has never bitten, and that is the argument FOR fixing it, not against:** the `is_demo` stop fires earlier on every send path, so this guard has never been the one holding — but **a backstop that only holds while the thing in front of it holds is not a backstop**, and the entire purpose of this function is to be correct when it is the only line left. A demo address reaching Resend is a hard bounce on the sending domain **every real client shares**. Matched on the domain suffix rather than a substring, because `invalidations@acme.com` is a real mailbox and a sloppier match would silently stop mail to a paying client. The test is pinned against `MBF_MARKER` from `demo-mbf-data.ts` rather than a literal, so renaming the demo domain **fails the test instead of quietly reopening the hole**. The function had **no tests at all** — its only previous appearance in the suite was as a `vi.mock` that returned `true`, which is to say the one guard nobody had ever exercised; it now has 11, red proven (5 fail with the line removed). **Dot deliberately NOT flipped:** #544 is already 🩷, the demo is live, and this adds a guard rather than shipping the item — `flip-dots.sh` reported *0 flipped*, which is the script's own idempotency rule agreeing. **1193 across 73 files** (1182/72 before), gate 6/6 green. One file, nine lines.
- **29 Jul (⚖️ #410 — four legal pages, three different answers about who holds your prospects' data):** the disagreement was not one-way. `privacy.html` named **Apollo only** in four places and `dpa.html` named Apollo only in the **formal table a client's counsel reads**, while `terms.html` named **PDL + Hunter and omitted Apollo** — so whichever page a client opened, the disclosure was wrong, and under GDPR/POPIA an incomplete sub-processor list is not a copy problem but a failure to disclose who personal data is shared with. **`dpa-us.html` carried the Apollo-only list too and I walked straight past it on the first pass** — caught only because the inventory row named it, which is a decent argument for reading the row before believing the prompt's file list. **The list is derived from code rather than assumed:** PDL is PRIMARY (`PDL_API_KEY` = *"important · PRIMARY lead sourcing"*), Hunter does **work-email verification** in the `enrichment.ts` waterfall — it does not prospect, so calling it a "data source" would misdescribe what it actually receives — and Apollo is wired in `apollo.ts` but its key is marked *"optional / BYO-key; not used in the day-to-day PDL+Hunter stack"*, with `docs/legal.md` saying flatly *"K.I.N.D no longer uses Apollo"*. **Apollo stays on the list, qualified as "used only where a Client configures it":** it can receive data the moment a key is set, and **omitting a processor that may receive data is the dangerous direction of this error** — over-disclosure costs nothing, under-disclosure is the breach. 22 tests read the real HTML and fail if the four pages ever disagree again; red proven by restoring the Apollo-only lists (8 fail, including the cross-page consistency assertion). **And one thing I caught in myself:** I first wrote *"Hunter — GDPR compliant, EU-based"* into a **legal** document on my own knowledge, with nothing in the repo verifying it. Jurisdiction decides whether SCCs are required, so I stripped the unverified claim from the prose and flagged the two Location cells I did have to fill (PDL: United States · Hunter: European Union) as **needing lawyer confirmation under #432**, which already tracks the outstanding PDL+Hunter data-rights review. Copy only, no layout change. **1182 across 72 files** (1160/71 before), gate 6/6 green. ⚠️ **Live marketing site — deploys on merge, preview first (RULEBOOK §11), and these are contractual documents.**

Dot is 🟡 and the owner moved 🤖→🤝. **A drift guard ships with it:** the copy quotes $99/100/$4 while those numbers live in `onboarding-pack.ts`, so a test asserts the sentence matches `PACK_PRICE_USD`/`PACK_LEADS`/`LEAD_PRICE_USD` — drifting against a price change is precisely how this string became a lie in the first place.
