- **29 Jul (🚪 #560 PR 2 of 2 — "public" now has to be a decision, and the test found two routes nobody had named):** three portal routes were reachable with no session and no comment. **The defect was never that they are public — all three should be.** It is that nothing distinguished *"we decided this is public"* from *"nobody thought about it"*, and those two are indistinguishable in the one file whose job is deciding who gets in. That is not hypothetical: **#478 found `partner-preview` — a fake partner dashboard — exposed to the whole internet by exactly that shape.** All three are now declared in `middleware.ts` with reasons: `consent` (a prospect with no account, arriving from the POPIA email; the `?token` is the credential and gating it breaks a *legal requirement*, not a feature) · **`invite/accept`, whose reason is the non-obvious one and the one most likely to be "fixed" into a bug** — a middleware redirect to `/login` would **drop the `?token` from the URL, destroying the invite by the very act of protecting it**; the page reads the session itself and renders *login-required*, and the API takes the accepting user from the auth token rather than a spoofable param (#266) · `share/[token]` (public IS the feature — an unguessable token so a client's boss can read a report without an account). **The enforcement is the actual deliverable:** a test walks the real app directory and **fails the gate** if a route is neither gated nor declared, so the next person in a hurry cannot add a public page silently — red proven by adding one and watching it fail with an actionable message. **And it immediately earned its keep by finding two routes nobody had named:** `book/[token]`, which is correct and now declared (a prospect books a meeting on an HMAC-signed 90-day token, #361/#368), and **`/billing/confirm` — the PAYSTACK return URL for a processor deleted outright in #352.** Stripe returns to `/dashboard/billing?stripe=success` instead, so nothing has routed there for weeks while it sat publicly telling anybody who typed the URL *"Payment received"*. **It hid because it lives inside the `(dashboard)` route GROUP** — parentheses mean the folder contributes nothing to the URL, so its real path is `/billing/confirm` with **no `/dashboard` prefix**, and the gate that looks like it covers that tree never touched it. A route group is invisible in the URL and that is genuinely easy to miss when reasoning about prefixes. Now redirected. ⚠️ **Left for the founder rather than actioned:** the only UI that hands a client a share link lives in `(dashboard)`, which middleware redirects to `/milla` for every signed-in client — so **no new share link can be generated today while old tokens still resolve**. Retiring it would break any link a client has already sent, which is a founder decision, not a middleware one. `(dashboard)` is redirected and **never deleted** (CORE-MAP: Milla's billing page is a 13-line wrapper around that tree). The declarative list deliberately does **not** short-circuit the middleware — an early return would skip the session lookup that `/` and `/login` both need. 26 tests. **1124 across 69 files** (1098/68 before), gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (✂️ #560 PR 1 of 2 — the website goes 28 pages → 12, and the two front doors nearly split):** sixteen pages retired, and the reasons are specific rather than a cull: **`figsy`** sold the engine as a standalone product, which the 22-Jul pivot explicitly ended (*FIGSY is the engine UNDER Milla&Vida*) — 73KB selling something you cannot buy; **`nexus`** sells a feature that is built, **default-OFF and fenced**; **`status`** is a status page with **nothing monitoring production** (#199 — the health probe exists and no monitor points at it), and a status page that cannot tell the truth is worse than none; `help-centre` overlaps `support`; `pipeline-calculator` and `vs-hiring-an-sdr` are pricing questions; `about`; and `the-drop` + eight episode pages. **Kept 12:** the buyer's path (index · pricing · milla · vida · demo · solutions · support · trust) plus all four legal pages. **Nothing was deleted** — CORE-MAP rule 3 is founder-locked, so every file stays on disk and each retirement is a single reversible line; the prompt offered *"301 or delete"* and 301 is the option that honours the lock. **The defect this nearly shipped is the one worth recording: the site has TWO front doors.** Express on Railway (`server.js`) and Cloudflare Pages (`_redirects`) — and `_redirects` is a Cloudflare file, **completely inert under Express**. Retiring at the CDN alone would have left all sixteen pages fully reachable on the primary host, with which one a buyer got depending on where DNS pointed that week. Two files each correct on their own, the bug living in the gap — **the exact shape of `normalisePort` (#547)**, where a tested guard sat unused next to a transport doing its own `Number()`. Both lists now exist and **a test reads the real files and fails if they drift**, plus a guard that the `/*` catch-all stays LAST, because Cloudflare takes the first match and a `/*` above the 301s would silently swallow every one of them. The nav and footer were **byte-identical across all 28 pages** (checked by hashing before touching anything), so the rewrite was one exact replacement verified by tag balance; three body CTAs to Nexus were removed and a per-page test asserts **no kept page links to a retired one** — a link that 301s is not broken, but it is a dead end mid-journey and reads as a broken site. **The test caught me twice again:** it found the three Nexus body links my nav surgery missed, and my own ordering assertion was anchored on the bare string `express.static`, which also appears in the comment explaining why the order matters — so it was asserting the redirect came before its own rationale, which is always true. Re-anchored on `app.use(express.static`. **Verified live rather than claimed:** started the server and curled it — 10/10 retired paths 301 to the right target, 12/12 kept pages 200. 24 tests, red proven (splitting the two lists and hoisting `/*` fails 4). **1122 across 69 files** (1098/68 before), gate 6/6. ⚠️ **This is the live marketing site — it deploys on merge and MUST be previewed first (RULEBOOK §11).** PR 2 (portal gating) still to come.
- **29 Jul (📭 #552 — a documented gate that did not exist, decided rather than left ambiguous):** LAUNCH-PAD said *"Start work refuses without an inbox"*; `start-work.ts` gated on payment, ICP and user and **not** on a mailbox, with the only refusal firing at send time. One of the two was wrong and nobody had chosen — which is worse than either behaviour, because the doc was teaching an operator a rule the product did not enforce. **Decided: warn loudly, do not block**, and the reasoning is the shape of the two halves rather than a preference. Sourcing spends OUR data budget to fill a desk the client's **$99 has already bought**, and the mailbox is a separate purchase *we* make days later — so a hard refusal there idles a paying client waiting on us, which is the opposite of fail-closed's intent. Sending touches a real prospect from a real mailbox, and that is where fail-closed already lives and stays: `figsy.ts` refuses, rolls the step back, and has nothing to fall back to. So the gate stays where the danger is, and this makes the state **visible** instead of leaving an operator to discover it when the first send silently defers. **The design point worth keeping: it reuses `resolveSendingInbox`, the send path's own resolver.** A hand-rolled *"does a `client_inboxes` row exist?"* check is the obvious implementation and it is wrong — it would drift the instant the send rules changed, and Vida would show a green light over a **warming** mailbox (sending on it is what un-warms it), a row with **no SMTP credentials**, or a missing **`INBOX_SECRET_KEY`**. Three separate refusal reasons and **not one of them is "no row"**. **A failed check reports UNKNOWN rather than a green light** — "the check broke so assume it's fine" is the exact failure this week has been spent removing — and still does not block, because a warning that breaks the thing it annotates is worse than no warning. Vida renders it **amber, deliberately**: green buries it, red reads as *"the sourcing failed"*, which is a different and wrong instruction to an operator deciding what to do next. The `source_run` audit row records `cannot_send`, so *"we sourced 200 for a client who could not send"* is answerable later. 15 tests, **red proven** — swallowing the warning and green-lighting a broken check fails 10. ⚠️ **Found while in the file and deliberately NOT fixed:** `/operator/source` reads active ICPs with `.eq('is_active', true).maybeSingle()` — **the #571 defect living in a second place**; with two active ICPs PostgREST errors, `icp` comes back null, and the route 400s *"No active ICP"* at a client who has two. Logged on #552's row; it is its own shippable change. Also confirmed pre-existing and untouched: `apps/admin` carries 11 `tsc` errors on clean `main` (re-export wrappers) — the gate builds admin with `next build`, which passes. **1098 across 68 files** (1083/67 before), gate 6/6 green. **Deploy note: `apps/admin` is the operator console — live on merge.**
- **29 Jul (🔇 #564 residual — the pattern the item condemned, surviving inside the item that condemned it):** #564 fixed `setCampaignStatus` and left `startCampaign` carrying the identical defect *verbatim*, down to the comment: parse the response, throw it away, empty `catch { /* surfaced by the reload */ }`. It never was surfaced — the reload re-rendered the same blocked state, so a refusal and a success looked exactly alike. **And it is the *"Just unblock them"* button**, which only appears when a client has **no campaign and therefore cannot be worked at all** — approvals blocked, the $4 deliberately not charged — so it is the worst possible place for "nothing happened" to be unreadable: an operator who believes they fixed it walks away and leaves the client stuck. It now surfaces refusals and network reasons the same way its sibling does, and **stops claiming to have started something that already existed**: the route returns `created: false` when the client already had an active campaign, and reporting that as "started" is a smaller version of the same lie. ③ was verified rather than assumed — a guard reads `/campaign/start` and asserts it records `start_campaign` and never `pause_campaign`. **The test caught my own sloppiness twice, which is the part worth keeping:** a fixed-length source slice ran past the function, and slicing to the next `async function` still swallowed *that* function's leading comment — and `setCampaignStatus`'s comment **quotes "surfaced by the reload" while explaining the original defect**, so both bounds would have had the guard passing or failing on a comment *about* the bug rather than on the code. It failed on its own first run and I bounded it at the function's own closing brace. A guard that measures the wrong text is the same family as a check that reports OK without running (#579, #581). **Dot is 🟡, not 🩷, and deliberately so: ② is still open** — `page.tsx:556` fires Run with **no confirmation** (one click begins emailing real prospects) and silently no-ops when there is no campaign, having just told the operator the next action was "Run". Also noted while in the file, untouched: `:530` `catch { setAsks([]); setFromClient([]) }` is the #565 shape. 10 new guards, **red proven** (7 fail against the old body). **1083 across 67 files** (1073/67 before), gate 6/6 green. **Deploy note: `apps/admin` is the operator console — this goes live on merge and the founder is its only user.**
- **29 Jul (🕳 #571 — two caps that delivered less than the client paid for and reported success):** neither errored, logged, nor left an empty run to notice. ① sourcing read `.order('created_at', desc).limit(1)` — **only the newest active ICP was ever sourced**, so a client with two live ICPs had one ignored *forever*: they approved it, the board says "never sourced", and nothing in the product ever explains why. Vida's People tab renders an `icp_name` per person precisely because several ICPs are expected. ② `surfaceEverything` used `.limit(1000)` **inside a function whose entire contract is "everyone"** — past a thousand undecided leads the rest were never surfaced and never delivered, and the caller reported the truncated figure to the founder as *"sent N to them"*. **The split rule is now written down rather than left to be re-derived from the code:** evenly, because there is no basis for ranking one approved ICP above another — weighting by age or past yield would quietly turn a client's preference into our policy — remainder to the newest first, and a zero share skipped rather than issued as an empty run that still costs an API call and writes a confusing outcome row. **Sequential, not `Promise.all`, and that is the subtle one:** every run spends the same pre-funded allowance through `try_spend_sourcing`, so firing them together would race that check with two runs each reading "enough left" and both spending it. It is a background job off a webhook — nothing to gain from concurrency, a budget to lose. **One ICP failing now alerts even when another succeeded**, naming which and how many did land, because a partial delivery is otherwise indistinguishable from a normal slightly-small one. On ②, paged with the shared `pageRows` rather than given a bigger limit — a bigger limit is the same bug with a later trigger — and ordered by **`id`, not score**: paging needs a stable unique key, `score` is nullable with common ties, and the score ordering only ever decided *which* thousand survived a truncation that no longer exists. **A read failure now alerts instead of returning a calm zero** (`pageRows` throws where `.limit()` swallowed it into an `undefined`, and zero-surfaced reads exactly like an already-stocked client). One existing test was updated rather than deleted: it pinned the bare error string, and #571 prefixes the ICP id because "which one failed" is the first question — so it now asserts the intent plus the attribution, which is the lesson the RLS migration test already carries (*a guard should assert the intent, or it just freezes the defect*). 19 new tests, **red proven** by reinstating both caps (10 fail). **1073 across 67 files** (1054/66 before), gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (⏰ #491 — a dead cron was silent, and the hard part was the throttle):** `callInternal` already wrote every run to `cron_runs` and dead-lettered every failure — and **neither of those is a signal.** Both are PULL surfaces: true the moment they are written, read only when somebody thinks to look. So a job that started failing kept failing on schedule and the first person to find out would be the client whose leads stopped arriving. **Two detectors, because a scheduled job stops in two genuinely different ways:** ① it RAN AND FAILED, which leaves an `ok=false` row; and ② it **NEVER RAN AT ALL**, which leaves nothing for ① to fire on — that is the **claim-then-crash path #343 itself introduced**: a process claims the slot (the claim is an INSERT, so the slot is now taken), then dies before finishing, every other replica *correctly* stands down, and the job simply does not happen. The single-run guard working exactly as designed, producing a silent miss. Same shape as a dead scheduler or a `RUN_CRONS=false` left on by accident. **The alert was the easy half; the throttle was the work.** `/figsy/send-due-all` runs every two hours, so alerting on each failure sends twelve identical emails a day — and an alert that arrives twelve times is an alert that gets filtered, which is precisely how the NEXT real one is missed (the same reasoning that keeps `smartleadRefusalIsNews` quiet on its five expected refusals). Three rules that pull against each other, held at once: a failure **after a success** always alerts (a job that was working and now is not is the most actionable signal there is) · an **ongoing** outage stays quiet for 24h · but it **nags again** once the window passes, so silence is never mistaken for recovery. **No new infra, per the brief:** the throttle marker rides in `cron_runs.note`, which is already written and already read by the health page — and deliberately not an in-memory counter, because this process restarts on every deploy, which is exactly when a cron is most likely to be broken. **The stale detector measures each job's own median gap instead of carrying a list of "daily jobs":** a list lives in one file while the schedule lives in another and nobody updates two places, and it would have fired on all four genuinely weekly jobs — four false alarms being how an alert channel dies. Measured cadence means the weeklies exclude themselves and an hourly job that stops is caught with no configuration at all. It runs inside the existing daily prune job rather than a new timer, because a new scheduled process is one more thing that can itself die silently. **And the branch that matters most: a history read failure ALERTS rather than staying quiet** — unable to evaluate the throttle must mean "tell them", never "say nothing". 25 tests, **red proven** by deleting the throttle and the weekly exclusion (3 fail, including the weekly false-alarm guard). **1054 across 66 files** (1029/65 before), gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (📮 #547 — the last shared sender to a real prospect, and a decision that was overdue):** #547 moved every sequence step onto the client's own mailbox and **left `manual-reply.ts:54` on `COLD_FROM`**. So the state it shipped into was worse than either extreme: the machine-written steps left the client's mailbox and the **human reply in the same conversation left the shared address** — the prospect watched the sender change mid-thread, and every client's manual replies pooled their spam complaints onto one domain, which is RULEBOOK 12.2 exactly. It now runs the same resolve-or-refuse as the sequence path. **The refusal is the feature:** a 409 carrying the operator-readable reason, because a human clicked Send and is owed the sentence — and because a silent fallback looks like success while the damage lands on every other client weeks later, where nobody connects it back. A mail-server rejection returns **502** rather than the old `throw sendError`, which surfaced as a bare 500 that could not tell you whether the mail had left. **`COLD_REPLY_TO` went with it, deliberately:** pointing replies at our shared address would send the prospect's next message to **us** instead of the mailbox holding the conversation, defeating the inbox→client→lead routing #551 had just built, and a From/Reply-To split across two domains is its own spam signal. **Red proven** — simulated the fallback and watched 8 tests fail, including *"NEVER falls back to the shared address"*, then restored. **The decision that had been left hanging: the CONSENT email stays OURS.** It is our mail as the processor ("on behalf of X via K.I.N.D"), it carries the POPIA audit trail and our own consent links, and the deciding reason is that it is the one message sent **before consent exists** — putting pre-consent mail on a client's freshly-warmed branded mailbox is precisely what un-warms it, and it fires at sourcing time when a client may have no mailbox at all. 12.2 is not breached because the sender is us, identified as us. The reasoning is written into `email.ts`, not just here. ⚠️ **One sub-question left open on purpose:** consent mail leaves from the **transactional** identity while D4 forbids cold-adjacent mail poisoning that domain — moving it touches the domain every invoice and password reset uses, so it gets its own PR and the founder's call. **And a measurement correction worth keeping:** I have twice quoted suite totals from an `apps/api`-scoped run while calling them the gate's. The gate runs vitest from the **repo root**, which also picks up `apps/portal/src/lib/onboarding-machine.test.ts` (14 tests) — so the real figures are **1029 across 65 files (1009/64 before this branch)**, not the 995/63 quoted on the #349 PR. Gate 6/6 green.

So a failed insert meant the $4 was gone, the lead read as **UNPAID**, and the next enrol path through it charged the client **a second time, silently**. `approve-lead.ts:258` was fixed for exactly this defect in the wallet-approve path and **this door was left swallowed** — the same bug, one function away, surviving the fix that named it. - **27 Jul (📬 #551 — REPLIES LAND BACK AGAINST THE RIGHT INBOX, built BEFORE the defect can bite):** the chain is now **inbox → client → lead → thread**. `InboundReply` carries `toEmail`, `resolveInboxOwner()` maps `client_inboxes.email` → client, and `routeReply()` keeps only that client's matches. **The point worth holding on to is WHY this had to be built now:** today every reply arrives at one shared Resend inbox, so matching on the PROSPECT'S address and fanning out to every client holding that lead is **correct** — that is R1, and narrowing it would have started dropping replies the day it shipped. The moment a client sends from their own mailbox the reply arrives **there**, and the same fan-out becomes **one client reading another client's inbound mail**. So the fix is not a change of behaviour today; it is the routing being ready the hour the first mailbox is recorded, and a test asserts that an unknown inbox behaves **exactly as it does now**. **A known inbox with no matching lead returns EMPTY and alerts — it does NOT fall back**, because falling back is precisely the harm: handing a stranger's reply at Acme's mailbox to whichever other client happens to hold that lead. The status filter is **deliberately absent**: a released mailbox still receives mail for weeks and the pooled→branded handover overlaps by design, so filtering to `active` would silently orphan the tail of every switched-over client. A lookup failure falls back to the fan-out — an outage may cost precision, never a reply. **Second clause, and it is the one that would have bitten silently:** idempotency keyed on `svix-id`, which is a **Resend transport id**. Instantly and Smartlead send no such header, so the moment a second provider delivered replies the guard would key on an empty string, `isDuplicateWebhookEvent` would fail open on **every** event, and a retried hot reply would re-run the whole path — a second CRM deal, a second alert, a second counter bump. `replyEventKey()` prefers the provider's own message id and **namespaces it by provider**, because two providers can legitimately issue the same id and neither must ever suppress the other's reply. 25 tests, red proven by breaking the unknown-inbox fallback and the namespace. 997 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (📮 PROMPT 7 — SMARTLEAD FOR CLIENTS, THE EXACT MIRROR OF INSTANTLY, and nothing exercised against a live workspace):** built to the same three-file shape — `smartlead.ts` Phase 2 (create campaign · save sequence · add leads, appended below the untouched Phase-1 read-only client), `smartlead-map.ts` (pure gates + payloads) and `smartlead-send.ts` (the hand-off) — **and wired into `approve-lead.ts` in the SAME PR**, because Prompt 4's lesson was a client and a mapping that nothing called. **The property that matters is that the two gates are exact INVERSES:** `canPushToInstantly` refuses anything that is NOT the house client, `canPushToSmartlead` refuses anything that IS, and a unit test asserts *for any client, at most one route accepts*. That is what lets the money path attempt **both** pushes with no if/else — deliberately, because an if/else would put the routing decision in the money path where a future edit could quietly send a client's lead from our mailboxes; the mutual exclusion lives in the gate functions where it is tested. **Four things reading the real files changed:** ① `SequenceStep` has **`wait_days`, not `day`** — days since the PREVIOUS step, which is exactly Smartlead's `delay_in_days`, so it passes straight through while Instantly's cumulative `day` has to be accumulated; getting that backwards fires a whole sequence at once or stretches it over months. ② **Non-email steps are dropped**, or a LinkedIn step becomes an email to a real prospect in our client's name. ③ The sequence is saved **only on campaign create** — re-saving per approval overwrites the copy mid-flight and a prospect halfway through receives step 2 of a different email. ④ **A `smartlead.ts` already existed** (Phase-1 read-only, June) and I nearly overwrote it: it was read first and EXTENDED, and it carried real endpoint detail (`/email-accounts/?offset=0&limit=100`) that no doc page would give me. **Security detail specific to this provider:** Smartlead authenticates by **query parameter**, so the credential is in every URL and lands in any log that records one — `redact()` therefore scrubs the **URL-encoded** form as well, because a key containing `+` or `/` appears as `%2B`/`%2F` and slips past a plain replace. `ignore_global_block_list` and `ignore_unsubscribe_list` are both **false**: setting them true to get more leads through is emailing people who said stop. **NOT_POSSIBLE names four limits**, chief among them that the **sequence STEP shape is UNVERIFIED** — api.smartlead.ai and helpcenter.smartlead.ai both return 403 to this environment, exactly as developer.instantly.ai did — so the endpoint is confirmed and the field names are not, and that must be checked against a live workspace **before the first client send**. **53 tests**, red proven by deleting the house-client refusal (4 red, including *"NEVER BOTH — for any client, at most one route accepts"*). One of my own assertions read `out[0].body` where the field is `email_body`: `undefined` does not contain `{{`, so it **would have passed while asserting nothing** — caught only because the same test also checked the rendered text. 972 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧾 THE THREE OWED ITEMS CLOSED — plus a placeholder of mine that reached production):** carried since the Prompt 5 audit; each is the same defect in different clothes — a screen built to tell the truth telling something slightly untrue about itself. **① The ledger integrity row told the founder to run a migration he had already run.** It asked *"does the live ledger accept the wallet types?"* and answered by looking for a ROW of that type; none yet → *"CANNOT tell … run 20260726_wallet_tx_types."* He had run it, repeatedly. **It could not tell because it was asking the wrong question:** *has a row ever been written* is about HISTORY, *will the constraint accept this* is about the SCHEMA — and Postgres answers that directly from `pg_constraint`, before any payment exists. Now reads the constraint definition itself, with the row-based reading kept only as a fallback for when `pg_catalog` is unreachable. **A false instruction that is correctly ignored teaches you to ignore the screen**, which is worse than saying nothing. **② A 4xx from a MISSING route read as "answering".** The endpoint check counted any 2xx-or-4xx as healthy, which cannot distinguish *the route declined your request* from *there is no such route* — delete a route and Express's default handler returns `Cannot GET /operator/x`, reported **green**. All twenty routes were verified to exist, so it was right **by luck rather than by construction**, on the screen whose entire purpose is not being right by luck. Our routes answer JSON, Express's fallback answers HTML: that is the tell, and it is now the check. `/blockers`, `/bookings` and `/nexus` also gained the notes they never had — *"HTTP 404 — answering."* with nothing explaining why a 404 was acceptable is **a green the reader cannot check**. **③ The reply path was not on the System screen at all.** The check covered signup → pay → source → approve → send and stopped — yet a reply is the thing the client is BUYING, and the one step they cannot see failing, because *a reply that never arrives looks exactly like a prospect who never answered*. Three sessions of work went into that spine and none of it was reported. The new row is CHECKED-BROKEN when `RESEND_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is unset (every inbound reply rejected, silently) and explicitly says **configured is not the same as proven**. **④ And my own mistake.** I wrote a worked example for rewriting `DATABASE_URL`, with a placeholder ref, and it was pasted in literally — production came back `(ENOTFOUND) tenant/user postgres.abcdefghijk not found`, Supavisor being perfectly accurate and completely unhelpful: **it names a tenant, not a mistake.** `DATABASE_URL` and `SUPABASE_URL` sit beside each other in Railway and must describe the SAME project, so a disagreement is **always** a typo — now detected before any connection attempt, in all four paths that read the database, with one sentence naming the variable, the wrong value and the right one. Blast radius was checked before advising: `DATABASE_URL` is used only by the migration runner and the three admin probes, so **no client-facing path was affected.** 22 tests. 919 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🛑 CI HAS NEVER RUN. NOT ONCE. FOUND WHILE CLOSING PROMPT 6):** the eight Prompt 6 items were all merged and deployed and every one still sat at **🟡** — so I checked why the `inventory-autoflip` workflow had not flipped them, and the answer is that **GitHub Actions has never executed a single run on this repository.** All five workflows are registered and `state: active`; `test.yml`, `doc-lint.yml` and `inventory-autoflip.yml` each report **`total_count: 0`**. Almost certainly the same account flag that locks the Supabase dashboard (*"cannot authorize a third party application"*) — **one blocker sitting under a lot of items.** Three consequences, each of which changes what the docs mean: ① **CLAUDE.md said of doc-lint *"CI runs it too"* — it does not**, and that sentence has been reassuring for eighteen days; ② **the test suite has never run anywhere except my container and the founder's Mac**, which is exactly why the 26-Jul `loadError is not a function` incident got all the way to a deploy — *there was no second machine to catch it*, and it also means **`scripts/check.sh` is not a belt over CI, it IS the only gate**; ③ **every 🩷 dot in the inventory was set by hand**, so the documented merge→doc automation has been describing something that has never happened. Corrected both claims in CLAUDE.md rather than leaving them to be believed, and flipped the eight Prompt 6 items 🟡 → 🩷 manually (🟢 stays founder-only). **The `Flips:` lines stay in PR bodies** — they cost nothing and start working the day Actions does. **This is the fifth instance this week of a check that reported a pass it had never performed** (`count-inventory.sh --check` falling through, the ledger integrity row advising a migration already run, the Engine panel showing eleven successes as one failure, an RLS verdict read from drifted files) — and the largest, because it was the check over all the others.
- **27 Jul (💾 #298 — THE DRILL THAT CANNOT BE RUN, AND THE PIECE THAT WAS MISSING ANYWAY):** plan written; **the drill itself remains undone and the document says so in its first line.** A restore drill is *take a backup → restore it somewhere → prove it matches*, and the middle step is impossible: the **Supabase dashboard is unreachable** (GitHub OAuth, flagged account), so there is no way to see the backups, trigger a point-in-time restore, or create the second project you would restore INTO — and there is no psql, no pg_dump and, by standing decision, no new tooling. **A confident runbook full of dashboard clicks nobody can perform is precisely the class of document this project keeps catching**, so it is not written. **But reading it end to end found something better: even if the dashboard opened tomorrow, the drill still could not have been done properly, because nobody knows what "restored correctly" looks like.** You press restore, the dashboard says success, and you have no way to tell whether you got everything, half of it, or last week's copy. **The comparison IS the drill. The button is not.** So `Vida → Engine → Backup manifest` now snapshots every table in `public` with its **exact row count**, and two choices in it are the whole point: a real **`COUNT(*)` rather than `reltuples`** — the planner's estimate is only as fresh as the last ANALYZE and is wrong by thousands on a **just bulk-loaded** table, which is exactly the state a restored database is in, so an estimate would **make a broken restore look fine**; and **counts only, never data** — a dump-everything endpoint would be one URL exfiltrating the entire customer database including `client_inboxes`, which **#554b found with no RLS at all** three hours earlier. The comparison is **exact, with no tolerance**: a restore landing 99% of `credit_transactions` has lost somebody's money and a percentage would hide it. Differences sort **money and people first** (`opt_out_blocklist` is in that list — losing it means emailing people who told us to stop). And step 6 of the runbook names the **most likely silent failure**: `smtp_pass_enc` restores perfectly as ciphertext whether or not the live `INBOX_SECRET_KEY` still decrypts it, so a restore can look complete and leave mailbox rows that exist and cannot send. **What is NOT proven is listed as its own section** — that backups exist, that a restore completes, how long recovery takes, that restored data is usable — because *"we rely on Supabase's automated backups, which we have never seen, verified, or restored from"* is the honest position and the exposure #298 was raised to name. **17 tests.** 897 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧹 #329 — THE SEED WIPE THAT MOSTLY SHOULD NOT HAPPEN):** plan + read-only report built; **execution stays founder-only**, per the clause *"executed only on my go"*. **The honest headline is that most of the item is already done, and not by deleting anything.** #329 was logged **7 Jul** and its stated purpose is *"so Finance/Sales/GTM read the real first client cleanly"* — but **#543 (demo exclusion)** and **#453 (demo mode)** were built afterwards, and the money surfaces already strip demo + house through `getClientExclusions()` (`internal-briefs`, `money-path`, `internal`). Its *"zero sample `deal_registrations`"* describes **rows that do not exist**: that table has exactly one writer (`routes/partners.ts`) and **no seeder at all**. **Exclusion is strictly better than deletion for this purpose** — reversible, cannot destroy something that turns out to have been real, and it keeps the demo working. So anything still leaking demo data into a real figure is **a missing exclusion to fix, not rows to destroy.** **Four protections, checked in a deliberate ORDER which IS the safety property: real money outranks `is_demo`.** A Stripe-referenced ledger row is a **fact**; the flag is a **human's opinion**; when they disagree on a destructive path the fact must win — so a paying client is protected **even if flagged demo, even if named `TEST DELETE ME`**. Then real leads (a desk someone has worked), then the house account, then the demo — **identified by the FLAG, never the name**, because `MBF Holdings` vs the live `MBF Demo` has bitten twice (#584/#582) and on a destructive path a name match deletes the wrong account. **MBF is kept on purpose: a wipe "at go-live" taken literally deletes the sales tool on the day it is most needed**, and `demo-mbf.ts` rebuilds it to a fixed cast anyway. Real payments are counted from `PURCHASE_TX_TYPES` and **not** `PAID_TX_TYPES` — a founder `manual_grant` must not make a test account permanently undeletable, which is the wrong kind of safe. **The arming gate expires:** `SEED_WIPE_ARMED` must equal **today's UTC date** AND the phrase must be typed exactly, so a variable left set in Railway cannot become a loaded gun a later deploy pulls — `FOUNDER_FLIP=1` is fine for a reversible dot, this is not reversible. The runbook's step 0 is *do nothing yet* (onboard the client first; the wipe is cleanup, never a prerequisite), and it refuses to proceed without **#298**'s backup drill, because *an untested restore is not a rollback plan*. Most likely outcome when the founder presses it: **clean**, and #329 closes without a single row deleted. **30 tests, every one about what must SURVIVE** — a wipe script's tests are not about deletion. 880 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧯 #554c — MY MIGRATION FAILED IN PRODUCTION, AND THE SCREEN REPORTED ELEVEN SUCCESSES AS "FAILED"):** the founder ran the migrations and got `relation "public.lead_enrichment" does not exist`. **My bug: `DROP POLICY IF EXISTS <policy> ON <table>` guards the POLICY, not the TABLE** — the table reference is resolved first, so a missing table is a hard error, and node-postgres sends a multi-statement query as **one implicit transaction**, so that first line rolled back all eighteen statements. It also answered the audit's open question: **most of those tables were never created in production at all** — they come from `packages/db/src/migrations/`, a directory nothing has ever confirmed was run there, which is exactly why the live audit found none of them exposed. Rewritten as a `do $$` loop guarded on `to_regclass(...) is not null`, which returns NULL instead of throwing for an unknown name, and which `raise notice`s each table as secured or skipped. **The second defect was worse than the first.** The Engine panel printed `Failed: …` and nothing else — but the runner opens a **fresh connection per migration** and catches per migration, so one bad statement stops nothing: **eleven migrations had applied and the screen rendered them as a flat red failure**, on the one screen whose job is telling the founder what the database now has. Same class as #565 and the leads banner — a real outcome collapsed into the scariest available word. It now reads *"11 of 12 applied … FAILED: … The rest DID apply."* **And a third, in my own test:** the guard for this migration pinned the exact literal `DROP POLICY IF EXISTS "Service role bypass" ON public.lead_enrichment`, so **fixing the production bug broke the test written to protect it** — a guard must assert the INTENT or it just freezes the defect. Rewritten to assert the tables are covered, plus two new tests that the DROP is guarded on table existence at all. Caught by the gate, not by review. 852 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🚨 #554b — THE LIVE AUDIT FOUND THREE TABLES, AND NONE OF THE FIVE I PREDICTED. `client_inboxes` HAD NO RLS AT ALL):** the founder ran the new checker against production at 11:12 and it returned **`client_inboxes` (RLS OFF)**, **`app_migrations_applied` (RLS OFF)** and **`agreement_templates`** — while every one of the five tables the repo-based verdict named came back clean. **The file verdict was wrong in BOTH directions**: it named five tables that are fine and **missed the most sensitive table in the product.** That is the entire justification for reading `pg_catalog` instead of files, delivered within an hour of building it. **`client_inboxes` holds `smtp_pass_enc`, `smtp_host`, `smtp_user` and `email` — how we log in to send as each client — and RLS was never enabled on it ANYWHERE in the repo**: `20260725_client_inboxes` created the table and simply never included the line, so there was **nothing for a file-based audit to read**. Anyone holding the public anon key could read every client's mailbox configuration. The passwords are AES-256-GCM ciphertext (`lib/inbox-secret.ts`) so this was never plaintext credentials, but hosts, usernames and addresses are plain and the ciphertext should not have been fetchable either. `app_migrations_applied` was created as a **side effect** of `20260724_one_wallet.sql` via `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so it never passed a review that would have asked. And **`agreement_templates_admin_write` exists ONLY IN PRODUCTION** — the repo has never contained it, someone created it by hand — which is **#558 pointing the other way: production carries policies no file can show you**, so every security review this project has ever done was blind to them. Closed by `20260727_rls_live_findings` (two ENABLEs and one DROP POLICY — no data, no columns). **Safe because nothing in a browser touches any of the three — and proving that corrected a mistake of my own:** `BROWSER_READ_TABLES` had thirteen entries because the grep counted **Next.js server route handlers**, which build their client with `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` and bypass RLS entirely. Re-derived from client components only it is **five** tables. Counting server routes is the dangerous direction — it makes a table look like it NEEDS a browser-facing policy when it does not, which is exactly how a `USING (true)` survives review. 850 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🔐 #554 — RLS READ END TO END: #350 FIXED ONE LEAK AND LEFT THE CLASS; THIS FOUND FIVE MORE):** read **151 policies across 39 SQL files**. The inventory row said *"seven migration files touch row-level security"* — the surface is **five times** what the row claimed, which was the first finding. **Two Postgres rules produced every other one.** ① **A policy with no `TO` clause applies to PUBLIC** — every role, including the `anon` role the public Supabase key authenticates as, which ships in the browser bundle. So `CREATE POLICY "Service role bypass" … FOR ALL USING (true)` restricts **nothing** to the service role: **the name is the trap**, because reviewers read names and not missing clauses. ② **Permissive policies combine with OR**, so one `USING (true)` makes every careful `client_id = current_client_id()` policy on that table irrelevant — **the weakest policy on a table IS the policy.** #350 found exactly one instance (`visitor_sessions.admin_read_visits`, visitor IPs + enrichment, **confirmed in prod**), dropped it, and nobody swept the pattern. **The sweep found five more, every one created by a migration in the PRODUCTION path and dropped NOWHERE in that path** — the only DROPs sit in `supabase/staging-schema.sql`, a *staging* snapshot: **`lead_enrichment`** (enrichment on real people — the closest thing to `visitor_sessions`), **`partner_commissions`** (what each partner is owed), `partner_referrals`, `partners`, `figsy_calls`. Closed by `20260727_rls_close_public_policies`, which drops **policy definitions only** — not a row, not a column (NOTHING GETS DELETED) — and is safe because dropping a permissive policy can only ever *remove* access, the API uses the service-role key which bypasses RLS entirely, and **no browser code reads any of those tables**, verified by reading every `.from('…')` in the portal, website and admin. **The verdict is also LIVE, and that is the point of the item:** this repo has **three migration directories and two whole-schema snapshots that disagree with each other**, and #558 is the standing finding that none describes production — so a verdict read off a file is a verdict about a file. `Vida → Engine → Run RLS audit` reads `pg_policies` and `pg_class.relrowsecurity` straight out of the live database and applies the same classification; where the document and the endpoint disagree, **the endpoint wins**. A failed audit renders as an **error, never an empty pass** — "nothing found" because the query died is the worst lie available on a security screen. Note `pg_class` is queried alongside `pg_policies` deliberately: **a table with RLS switched OFF is the most dangerous state there is and has no `pg_policies` row to betray it.** **32 tests.** Two of my own scripts were wrong mid-audit and caught by checking rather than reporting: the first policy regex used `[^"\s]+` and silently dropped every multi-word policy name, mislabelling tables as "no policies"; and my own explanatory comment containing `ON t FOR ALL USING (true)` invented a phantom table `t` in the scan. 844 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (💳✂️ #352 — A CARD CHARGE ANY PROSPECT COULD TRIGGER BY REPLYING TO AN EMAIL, REMOVED):** founder-confirmed *"I confirm: yes, remove"*. Reading the block end to end found **four faults stacked in one `try`**: ① it charged in **ZAR at a rate we invented** — `Math.round(amountUsd * 19 * 100)`, a hardcoded USD→ZAR 19 inside a live card charge, so a $20 bundle billed **R380** whatever the rate actually was; ② **check-then-act**, which is the AR-14 headline — the "cooldown" COUNTED recent top-up rows and *then* charged, so two hot replies arriving together both counted zero and both charged (the real double charge `20260702_webhook_idempotency` describes; svix idempotency only ever stopped IDENTICAL replays); ③ it **could never succeed anyway** — the gate required `auto_topup_paystack_auth` and #325 pulled Paystack from the billing UI, which the code itself admitted in a comment reading *"Landmine: unreachable until a Paystack auth exists"*; ④ **nothing received the result** — `index.ts` mounted raw-body parsing for `/webhooks/paystack` and **no route was ever registered behind it**. A payment integration that cannot complete, in a currency we do not bill, at a rate we made up, with a race condition. **Removing it settles AR-14 outright instead of hardening it — there is no charge left to race** — and, the part worth remembering long after Paystack is forgotten, it takes a **payment out of a webhook any prospect can trigger by replying to an email**. **What was deliberately NOT removed**, because the founder authorised removing the CHARGE PATH and not the history (NOTHING GETS DELETED, 26 Jul): the client's stored `auto_topup_*` preferences, the columns behind them, and every historical Paystack reference in `credit_transactions` — deleting billing history to tidy a code path would be indefensible. Milla's billing page has said *"coming soon"* since #325, so no client is promised anything that has now vanished. **Three more places still named Paystack as live**, all corrected: the founder brief reported Stripe's fees as **`paystack_fee_estimate_usd`** (the arithmetic was right, the NAME was a lie on a money report — now Stripe's real 2.9% + $0.30, the fixed leg no longer quietly dropped); the Vida health page monitored `status.paystack.com`, a processor we do not use; and the launch smoke test told an operator to verify *"Paystack opens (live key)"* — a checkout that no longer exists, which they would have reported as broken. **13 regression guards, 10 proven red** — the assertion is about what is no longer in the tree, so they read the real files, and three of them assert what must **survive**. One of my own guards fired on the legitimate `api.resend.com` reply-body fetch and was narrowed to money movement rather than a hostname: **a guard that fires on the wrong thing is worse than none.** 812 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (⌛ #342 — THE LAPSE CRON HAS 500'd EVERY DAY SINCE IT WAS WRITTEN, so nothing has ever lapsed):** `/subscriptions/check-lapsed` does `.update({ status: 'lapsed' })` and **`lapsed` is not in the production `subscription_status` enum** — Postgres rejects it, the handler rethrows, the route 500s, daily. **No subscription has ever been marked lapsed, so an unpaid client keeps access forever.** Third in the family after #190 (`paused`) and #340: a green unit test proves the code does what it says, and the database says no. **But adding the value was the easy half.** Make that write succeed and it starts locking accounts on the strength of `current_period_end` — a column **only our own webhook handler writes**. One missed `customer.subscription.updated` (a deploy mid-delivery, a 500, a retry we 200'd) and the column goes stale while the client pays perfectly well: the blind bulk update would have **locked out a paying customer using our own bookkeeping error as the evidence.** So a Stripe-managed subscription is now **never** lapsed here — Stripe reports `past_due`/`unpaid`/`canceled` itself and since #340 we store those faithfully — and one drifting >3 days past its period end raises a **missed-webhook alert** instead, which is a statement about our plumbing rather than an accusation against the client. What genuinely needed this cron is the other kind: **hand-granted subscriptions from Vida**, which nothing else in the system will ever expire. `'lapsed'` also added to the reactivation list, or a client who pays after lapsing stays locked out forever — the same bug pointed the other way. **Two more found reading the handler end to end:** the whole thing returned early on `!lifecycleEmailsEnabled()`, exactly like the six other lifecycle crons — but those only send mail and **this one changes billing state, so turning off marketing email silently switched off billing enforcement**; the email is gated now, at the point the email is sent, and the lapse always runs. And the daily 500 was only ever a `console.error` plus a dead-letter row — **a billing-enforcement job that has silently stopped working is precisely what must not wait to be noticed**, so it alerts. Also put `icps.pdl_scroll_token` and `cron_claims` on the System screen's REQUIRED_SCHEMA: neither #366's nor #343's migration could be *proven* applied, and that list is only ever as good as what is on it (#558's lesson, learned again when `copilot_mode` was missing while twelve rows showed green). **22 tests, 3 proven red** by reinstating the blind behaviour — including *"THE BIG ONE: a Stripe-managed subscription is left alone"*. 799 across the suite (785 API + 14 portal); gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (💳 #340 — A DECLINED CARD BOUGHT A WORKING PRODUCT):** one ternary in `routes/stripe.ts` — `sub.status === 'active' || sub.status === 'trialing' ? sub.status : 'active'` — and the false branch is the whole defect: **everything not already good was written as `active`.** Stripe's `incomplete` (card declined at signup, subscription never started), `past_due`, `unpaid`, `canceled`, `incomplete_expired` — all stored as a live paid subscription. Every consumer gates on `.eq('status','active')` or `.in(['active','trialing'])`, so **that line was the entire authorisation decision for the paid product, and it always said yes.** **It also undid the dunning:** `invoice.payment_failed` correctly writes `past_due` and alerts, and Stripe's paired `customer.subscription.updated` carried `past_due` straight back through the ternary and out as `active` — the one path built to catch non-payment, overwritten by the bug within the same second. **The fix had to avoid the trap that is literally the next item.** Production's `subscriptions.status` is a **Postgres ENUM**, not the text+CHECK the repo's `schema.sql` claims — #190 found this the hard way (*"invalid input value for enum subscription_status: paused"*) and **#342 is the same bug still live** for `lapsed`. So "map Stripe faithfully" is only half an instruction: writing `incomplete` into an enum that has never held it fails identically, and **a failed UPDATE leaves the row on its previous value — for an existing subscription, still `active`.** The honest fix would have recreated the bug it was fixing. Hence both halves: the migration adds the values, **and every mapping carries a `fallback` drawn only from values provably already in the enum that also deny access**, so the account stays locked in the window before the migration runs and the alert names the migration. Also: `canceled`→`cancelled` (Stripe one L, our enum two — a pass-through would have been #342 again); an **unrecognised** Stripe status **denies and alerts**, because the two ways to be wrong are not equal — a denied payer is visible in minutes, free access to an unmapped state is invisible until someone reads the revenue report; and renewal re-activation widened to `unpaid`/`incomplete`/`incomplete_expired`, which only became reachable once the real states started being stored, with `paused` deliberately excluded because a pause is the client's decision, not a payment failure. **30 tests, 5 proven red** by restoring the coercion for `incomplete` alone — including *"THE HEADLINE: a card declined at signup is not an active subscription"*. 777 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (⏱ #343 — TWO REPLICAS DOUBLED EVERY EMAIL AND EVERY CHARGE, and the env var the item asked for could not have stopped it):** `startCrons()` ran on every API process with no lock and no gate; the System screen had carried the warning for weeks — *"if replicas >1, every cron in cron.ts double-fires"*. **Built both halves, because reading the deployment made the obvious fix insufficient:** Railway sets variables **per SERVICE** and every replica inherits them, so `RUN_CRONS=true` on `@kind/api` reads `true` on *both* replicas and both fire. There is no per-replica override — a process can read its own `RAILWAY_REPLICA_ID` but never how many replicas exist, which is exactly why that System row is NOT-MEASURED. So `RUN_CRONS` is a **kill switch** and a way to keep crons off other services, and the guarantee lives where both processes can see it: **`cron_claims`, primary key (job, slot)**. The INSERT *is* the lock — first process wins, every other takes a unique violation and stands down; no advisory lock to leak on a dropped connection, no leader election, just a constraint Postgres already enforces perfectly. Same shape as the `(enrollment_id, step)` backstop from #354. **`slot` is ROUNDED to the nearest minute, not truncated** — two replicas run off two clocks, and truncation puts 07:59:59.8 and 08:00:00.2 into different slots so both claims succeed and the job runs twice: *the bug, reintroduced by a rounding choice*. **Unset means ON**, deliberately — failing closed would stop every send, digest, drip and charge in the business the moment a migration lagged a deploy, and silent total automation death is the worse failure; a missing claims table therefore **runs the job anyway and alerts loudly**, which is the entire safety net. **Two things found while reading `cron.ts` end to end:** the startup line reported **"25 jobs scheduled" while 33 were** — a number nobody recounts after adding a job, printed with total confidence, now counted from node-cron's own registry (verified at runtime, not assumed); and `checkSendsStalled` does not go through `callInternal`, so without its own claim two replicas would mail the founder the same stall every hour. The System screen's replica row was rewritten rather than left to become a stale claim on the one screen whose purpose is not making stale claims. **35 tests; proven red twice** — truncating instead of rounding (3 red, including *"replicas whose clocks straddle the minute boundary still collide"*) and treating a unique violation as anything but success (3 red, including *"THE SECOND STANDS DOWN — this is the whole item"*). 733 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (📄 #366 — A CLIENT'S SECOND MONTH COULD NEVER FIND A NEW PERSON: the repeat-business killer, fixed):** `pdlSearchPeople(icp, _page = 1, size)` accepted a page number and **ignored it** — the underscore was the entire defect. Every run asked PDL for the **same first page**; run two deduped every returned person against leads the client already held and inserted **nothing**, and the client was shown *"No leads matched this ICP"* — a sentence about their targeting that was actually a sentence about our paging. The cashflow (#556) says the pack is **−$52 in month one** and *"the repeat is the business"*; this was the code that made a repeat structurally impossible. **Fixed with `scroll_token`** (PDL v5's `from` is deprecated and now 400s), persisted per ICP so it **survives between runs** — a cursor that dies with the process pages nothing. **The trap that came with it:** a scroll token belongs to ONE query, so an edited ICP would replay a cursor into an audience the client no longer targets — *a working search delivering irrelevant leads*, worse than zero. The token is therefore stored with a **fingerprint of the query**, normalised so a dragged list or a capitalisation is not a change but a new country is. **The honesty half:** a finished audience and a broken run both arrive as `inserted: 0`, and told the wrong one a client either widens an ICP that was working perfectly or abandons one that simply ran to its end — **opposite instructions**. A new `audience_exhausted` outcome now says *"we have reached the end of this audience… to find more, widen the ICP"*, the founder is alerted **on the run that discovers it** (not every run thereafter), and the ICP stops spending grants re-asking a question already answered. Widening the ICP clears the exhaustion automatically — otherwise the silent zero returns through the other door. **Two defects found while wiring, both invisible to grep:** the portal's banner was an **allowlist of two statuses written into the JSX**, so the new outcome recorded a careful sentence in the database and rendered **absolutely nothing** — the silent zero one layer above its own fix; inverted in `@kind/shared` so every non-served status shows, **including ones nobody has written yet**. And `recordRunOutcome`'s `try/catch` caught nothing that actually happens — **supabase-js returns a constraint rejection rather than throwing**, so writing a fifth status into a four-value CHECK would have lost the outcome silently and left the portal showing the previous run's message: **#342's failure mode, fixed before it bit** by widening the constraint in the same migration and alerting when the DB refuses. 39 tests, the 7 wiring ones **proven red first**. 698 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🔎 PROMPT 5 — THE FULL AUDIT, THEN ALL SIX FINDINGS FIXED):** the audit walked signup → pay → source → approve → send → reply → book and stated coverage honestly at **3.1% of the core (1,590 of 51,470 lines)**, naming what it did not read: `routes/stripe.ts` (850), `routes/icps.ts` (1,159), `lib/figsy.ts` (~2,000), `routes/calendar.ts` (588). **Six findings, every one invisible to grep and found by reading a file end to end.** ① a client pays $99 and **their checklist never ticks** — the route counted `'purchase'` while Stripe writes `wallet_topup`; ② a failed count rendered as **0**, so *"we could not check"* read as *"you have not done it"* — #565's shape on the CLIENT's side; ③ `PORTAL_URL ?? 'http://localhost:3000'` put a **dead booking link in every cold email** (latent — the founder confirmed the var IS set); ④ a sourcing failure after payment returned `{started:true, sourced:0}` — **identical to already-stocked**, console-only; ⑤ **`mailer.ts` was the transport and ignored `normalisePort`**, the guard written for it, whose own comment warns a junk port is *"a connection attempt to nowhere"* — so a bad port hung every send for the full 20s timeout; ⑥ a database read failure was labelled *"No sending mailbox assigned"*, sending an operator to configure a mailbox that was already there. **All six fixed, 29 tests, every one proven RED first** — including `expected 'http://localhost:3000/book/…' to be null`, the exact string a prospect would have received. **Two of my own guards caught my own comments** quoting the old code; both strip comment lines now, because a check that forbids you describing what you fixed is a bad check. 659 across the suite. Minted #598.
- **27 Jul (🔌 PROMPT 4 WIRED — the missing hand-off built, and the whole thing BLOCKED on Instantly's billing):** the founder caught that Prompt 4 shipped the Instantly client and the mapping and **wired neither into anything** — the grep for callers returned *tests only*, while the PR read as complete. That grep now returns **`approve-lead.ts:318`**. `lib/instantly-push.ts` sits at **step 9**, after the enrol, because a lead must be paid for, revealed and in a sequence before anyone sends on its behalf; it is wrapped so it **cannot fail an approve the client has already been charged for**, and re-checks every gate itself rather than trusting the caller. **The house client is `HOUSE_CLIENT_ID`, an env var — not a name match**, because exact-string matching on a company name has bitten us twice (`MBF Holdings` vs the live `MBF Demo`, #584/#582): a name is a label a human edits, an id is not. **Unset means nothing is ever pushed** — fail closed, since guessing wrong means emailing real people from the wrong account. **Four refusals are deliberately silent** (demo · kill-switch · a client, who goes via Smartlead · no key) because alerting on the system working correctly would page the founder on **every approval** and train them to ignore it. **🛑 BLOCKED, not broken:** the founder created the key with the right four scopes and `GET /api/v2/accounts` returned **HTTP 402 Payment Required** — the plan/billing limit written into `NOT_POSSIBLE[]` on 26 Jul, now **confirmed rather than predicted**. Nothing is live-verified against Instantly and cannot be until the bill/plan is settled; founder-decided: *"wait for the bill to be paid, won't do this until the rest of the system is good."* Reply pulling and rate-limit backoff remain NOT-POSSIBLE. 630 across the suite.
- **27 Jul (🎨 P3-1 — EVERY ERROR IN THE VIDA COCKPIT RENDERED AS A SUCCESS: fifteen, not one):** the founder flagged the Run/Pause refusal; reading every setter and every render site under P10 showed it was **systemic**. One `saveMsg` string, **ten render sites all hardcoded `text-[#0e7c86]`** — teal — and **fifteen failure paths** writing into it: *"Could not save the ICP"*, *"Could not send the ask"*, *"Failed to load people"*, *"Could not update the booking"*, and the refusal added by #590. **An operator scans a console by colour before words**, which is exactly how fifteen failures sat unnoticed — on the screen whose whole prompt was about not lying, found while fixing that screen. **Fixed by making tone impossible to omit** rather than recolouring one line: `saveMsg` is a `{ text, tone }` pair, so a caller **cannot write a failure without saying it is one**, and the class comes from the tone instead of from whatever was copied into the nearest JSX. **The regression guard is the point:** unit tests prove the helper is right but can never prove the ten sites USE it — and that gap *was* the defect, because a correct colour was always available and the sites hardcoded one anyway. A test now **reads the page source** and fails on any teal `saveMsg` render, any raw `{saveMsg}` (which would print `[object Object]` at an operator), and any bare `setSaveMsg('Could not…')`; the admin app has no test runner, so it runs from the API suite. Proven red twice — collapsing the helper, and reverting **one** render site, which the guard names by line. Zero new type errors (11 pre-existing before and after). 591 across the suite. Minted #597.
- **27 Jul (📭 P2-2 — THE ALERT BLAMED THE PROSPECT WHEN THE FAILURE WAS OURS):** found by reading `figsy.ts` 198–240 end to end under P10. R3 already alerted — that half was built — but **the reason stayed in `console.error`**, and the alert said *"the body arrived empty"* for **four different situations**, describing three of them **wrongly**: the fetch returned **HTTP 500**; the fetch **threw**; **`RESEND_API_KEY` was unset so the fetch never ran at all** — and the alert still claimed it *"returned nothing"*; and the single case the wording actually fitted. **The distinction is the whole point:** an empty email is a prospect quirk to ignore, while HTTP 500 is **our pipeline down and every reply being lost** — told the wrong one, the founder ignores an outage. The route now carries the real reason out of the fetch block instead of logging it away (status **and** body text, or the thrown message verbatim), and `describeBodyFetch()` — pure and tested — turns it into a sentence naming what happened and **whose fault it is**. The missing-key reading is new, and is the one the old wording lied about hardest. **6 tests, all proven red** against the old single message, including one asserting the readings are **all different** — the fix stated as an assertion — and one that it **never blames the prospect when the failure was ours**. 582 across the suite. Minted #596.
- **27 Jul (🔁 P2-1 — THE AI CLASSIFIER WAS INSIDE THE PER-CLIENT LOOP, and I put it there fixing R1):** found by reading `figsy.ts` 127–505 **end to end** under the new P10, after the founder's diagnosis — *"you grep everything off last action and never read end to end"*. R1 wrapped the reply handler in a per-client loop so a prospect worked by two clients lands in **both** threads; `classifyReply` — an **Anthropic call taking only the body**, nothing client-specific — was inside it. Two costs per shared reply, and worse: **the model is not deterministic**, so the same email could come back **`hot` for one client and `warm` for the other** — one getting the founder alert, the web push and the CRM deal, the other getting none of it, **for the same email**. Hoisted above the loop. **Proven by the codebase's third route-level test**, because a unit test could never have seen it: `classifyReply` is correct, `findLeadMatches` is correct, and the reply spine already had 21 passing tests — **the bug was in the SHAPE of the route**, which is precisely what grep cannot show. Six tests drive the real Express handler: classify once for two clients · both threads still written (R1 not undone) · both rows carry the same classification · and an unmatched reply never reaches the classifier, so a stranger's email costs nothing. Red proven by putting the call back inside the loop → `expected 2 to be 1`. 576 across the suite. Minted #595.
- **26 Jul (📋 THE CLAUSE TABLE — the discipline made a required ARTIFACT rather than a promise):** founder, after catching Prompt 4 reported complete while nothing called it: *"your big issue is you grep everything off last action and never read end to end"*, then *"how do i now get you to do the same action on every new prompt"*. **P10 (read the path end to end) and P11 (every prompt answered with a clause table)** added to `docs/PRODUCT-RULES.md`, plus **§5a** carrying the exact wording the founder pastes **before** each work prompt — so it binds a fresh session with no memory of today. **Why an artifact, not a behaviour:** a behaviour is forgotten. The clause-by-clause read was committed to after Prompt 1 and **dropped by Prompt 4**. An artifact is enforceable without trust — a missing table means the PR comes back unread, exactly as the pasted-red rule already works. Three things the wording pins, each earned: the table is built **BEFORE** the work (so a clause like *"on approved leads, push the lead"* sits visible as a row from the start); it is **quoted, never paraphrased** (paraphrasing is how *"every /operator endpoint"* became *"the System screen"*); and **any ❌ goes in the first line and the PR title** (Prompt 4's NOT-POSSIBLE items sat near the bottom while the report led with what worked). **Every miss this week was invisible to grep** — a module nothing imported, a generator writing to a scratchpad that dies with the container, a green test *requiring* the value the database rejects, a refusal rendered in the success colour. Grep only ever returns what you already suspected. Minted #594.
- **26 Jul (🛑 THE GATE WAS GREEN FOR A REASON THAT DID NOT REPRODUCE — and the gate itself caught it):** the founder ran `ship.sh` and got `TypeError: loadError is not a function`, 21 tests failed, **NOTHING WAS DEPLOYED**. **The gate did exactly its job** — it refused to ship a red build to a live site. What it caught was **mine**. #590/#591 moved pure logic into `@kind/shared` so both consoles could share one copy; that package declares `"main": "./dist/index.js"` and **`packages/shared/dist` is gitignored** — a build artifact, not source — and `check.sh` never built it. So every check resolved `@kind/shared` to *whatever that machine last built*: fresh in the container it was authored in, stale on the founder's Mac. **The same commit was green in one place and 21-red in the other.** Fifth instance of the family: bash 3.2 (#578), BSD awk (#579), a hardcoded `/home/user/KIND` and a manifest the generator never wrote (#582) — every one tooling that works where it was written and nowhere else, and every one invisible from the container. **Fixed in both halves**, because either alone leaves a hole: `apps/api/vitest.config.ts` aliases `@kind/shared` to its **source**, so a unit test can never again depend on whether somebody ran a build; and **`check.sh` builds the package as step 1**, because the type-check and both Next builds legitimately consume `dist`. **Proven cold:** deleted the dist entirely → without the alias the founder's failure reproduces exactly → with it, **570 tests pass on an absent dist** → and the full gate from that same cold state is **6/6 green**. Minted #592.
- **26 Jul (💜 PROMPT 3, B of B — THE MILLA SCREENS THAT TOLD A PAYING CLIENT LESS THAN THE TRUTH):** split from #590 by **audience, not ticket** — this half is what a client sees, so the preview-first rule applies. **#563 — the billing page had no concept of the pack.** It read `/credits` and knew **only the wallet**, so a client who had just paid **$99** saw a **$0 balance and no mention of the 100 approvals they had bought**, on the one screen that exists to explain what they paid for. The cause is structural: the pack is a **counted quota, not a wallet credit** (#541) — the money sits in the *entitlement*, not the *balance* — so a wallet-only page could never show it. Now reads `pack` from `milla-summary`, the same `packState` the desk and greeting use, and a **failed** pack fetch says *"this does not mean you have none"* rather than rendering the wallet-only lie in a new hat. **#570, four desk gaps:** `Promise.all` **blanked the entire dashboard** when either endpoint failed, including the half that had loaded (the billing page already used `allSettled`; the desk did not) · **`pass()` never refreshed**, so the KPI still read *"3 leads awaiting"* after all three were passed, and with a 50-row cap passing one never pulled the next in · **the KPI counts every lead awaiting while the list is capped at 50**, so 120 waiting rendered as *"120 leads awaiting you"* above 50 rows with nothing explaining the other 70 — which reads as us having lost them · and **the 402 invented its figure in the browser** (`ids.length * 4`, blind to both the wallet balance and the leads still inside the pack), now using the server's numbers when sent and otherwise **stating the rule rather than a total we cannot stand behind** — a wrong number on a payment screen costs more trust than no number. **13 tests, all proven RED first**: the old arithmetic printed **$80** where the server had said **$34.50**. 570 across the suite. Minted #591 as **🟣**, not 🟡 — client-facing, so it wants eyes on preview before live. Gates: api tsc · full vitest · both consoles build · doc-lint — green.
- **26 Jul (🖥 PROMPT 3, A of B — THE VIDA CONSOLE LIED TWICE):** honesty fixes only, founder-authorised. **#564 — the audit log recorded `pause_campaign` for everything.** Pressing **Run it** wrote *"paused the campaign"*; so did a rename or a cap change. The audit log is the only record of who did what to a client's account, and on the most consequential action it said **the opposite of what happened** — worse than no log, because an empty log makes you go and look while a confidently wrong one makes you stop. Two new actions and a pure, tested `campaignAuditAction()` derived from what the patch actually does. **Second half: the refusal was never shown** — `setCampaignStatus` parsed the response and threw it away, so a refusing API produced a button that appeared to do nothing, while the comment claimed the failure was *"surfaced by the reload"*. Placed in `@kind/shared`, the one package both consoles can import, because the admin app has no test runner and a second copy is how the two would drift. **13 tests, both proven RED first.** Verified zero new type errors: admin `tsc` reports 11 pre-existing on unmodified main and 11 with these changes. 557 across the suite. **Split from the Milla half deliberately** — #563/#570 are client-facing and fall under the preview-first rule. Minted #590. Gates: api tsc · full vitest · both consoles build · doc-lint — green.
- **26 Jul (📬 PROMPT 2 — THE REPLY CHAIN: five defects fixed once, in the spine every provider will flow through):** founder-authorised, *"build them provider-agnostic"*. Resend delivers replies today; Instantly and Smartlead deliver them by API next under #577 — so the matching and opt-out logic moved into `lib/reply-ingest.ts` instead of staying inside the Resend webhook, where all three providers would have grown their own copy of the same five bugs. **R1, the worst: a reply matching leads at TWO clients was dropped forever.** `.eq('email', x).maybeSingle()` **errors** on more than one row, so `lead` came back null and the handler returned a silent 200 — and two clients prospecting the same person is ordinary, because the pool is shared. Every match is returned now and the reply routed into **each** client's thread; picking one would hand one client's reply to another, which is worse than dropping it. A failed lookup **throws** rather than returning empty, because "the query broke" and "nobody matched" must never look the same. **R2/R3:** an empty body or a failed Resend body-fetch both ended in the same silent 200 — Resend's `email.received` is metadata-only, so those two are indistinguishable. Both now alert **naming the provider message id**, so the reply can be opened in the provider and answered by hand. Nothing was ever lost; only the knowledge that it happened. **R6:** a hot reply sets the enrollment to `replied`, and the lookup filtered to `['enrolled','in_progress']` — so the follow-up *"actually, yes, let's talk"* skipped **every** hot path. Fixed, with a test pinning that the lookup and active-sending sets never collapse into one another. **R7:** the three opt-out writes were unchecked, and the blocklist is the single suppression source the send path reads — a silent failure means **we keep emailing someone who told us to stop**, the one failure here with a legal edge. All three checked, every failure collected, the alert **names the address**, and the blocklist is written first because the rest is bookkeeping. **21 tests, every defect proven RED against the old behaviour first.** 544 across the suite. Minted #589. Gates: api tsc · full vitest · both consoles build · doc-lint — green.
- **26 Jul (🧹 EVERY INTEGRITY CHECK NOW IGNORES OUR OWN INVENTED DATA — one shared rule, not six patches):** founder: *"yes i want perfect. wont settle for less"*. **Seven of the eight checks scanned every row in the database, counting demo accounts as real clients.** Nothing was wrong on the live run — all eight came back clean — but it is the class that produced **three false answers on the first run**, the worst a **CRITICAL** *"a client has paid and cannot be delivered"* about an account that **cannot send by design**. **The risk was never the wrong number; it is that the founder stops trusting the screen** — a check that cries wolf about invented companies is one you learn to ignore, and then it misses the real one. Fixed as **one rule**: the demo ids are read **once** per report and handed to every check, so eight checks can never disagree about which accounts are demos. `packAndWallet` had been running its **own** `is_demo` query — a second source of truth for the same fact — and now uses the shared set; exactly **one** `is_demo` lookup remains in the file. The judgement is pure and tested, including the two cases that decide whether it is safe: **no demos at all drops nothing**, and **all-demo yields clean rather than hidden**. `doubleCharged` had to start selecting `client_id`, because it reads only `reference` and so had **no way to tell a demo's charge from a real one**. **If the demo lookup itself fails the ENTIRE report renders UNANSWERED** — a green result would be a lie in both directions. **One check is deliberately exempt and says so in the code:** `ledgerTypesLive` asks whether the live CHECK constraint allows the wallet types, and a row proves it accepted them **whoever wrote it** — filtering would discard valid evidence. 26 → **32** tests in that file, **505** across the suite. It existed in exactly one place, the demo. **And `demo-mbf.test.ts` asserted `expect(kinds).toContain('objection')`** — a passing test *requiring* the value the database rejects, which is why this reached a live rebuild with a green suite behind it. **A test that pins invented data is worse than no test: it makes the wrong thing load-bearing.** Both values now match what the product writes; the objection story is untouched because it lives in the reply body, where it always did. New guard test pins every MBF classification to the union of the live constraint and what the unibox renders — **proven red on `'objection'`, green when restored**. **The principle, stated once:** a demo that writes values the real path never writes is not a demo of this product — it is a second, divergent implementation that happens to look similar, and it will keep breaking against constraints the real path satisfies. Minted #587. Gates: api tsc · full vitest (24 in this file) · both consoles build · doc-lint — green.

- **26 Jul (🔁 PROMPT 1 FINISHED AGAINST ITS OWN SPEC — the missing endpoint probe, three wrong answers, and a FOURTH check that was passing without running):** founder: *"is everything in that prompt right… because you claim stuff but it is never done"*, then *"d3 gap and d4 wrong. no i want everything in step 1 done right."* So I re-read the authorising prompt line by line instead of from memory. **The gap:** it asked for *"every `/operator` endpoint answering vs erroring"* and the System screen never called one — it probed dependencies, schema, clients and Vida state and skipped the routes every Vida screen is actually made of. `operatorEndpoints()` now calls **all 20 read-only operator GETs** over real HTTP against the running process; **5xx or no answer = BROKEN**, because a route that errors renders a Vida screen empty and *empty looks identical to "nothing to do"*. **The three wrong answers**, all from the first LIVE run and all the same shape — judgement inlined in a query where no test could reach it: the **demo** was reported CRITICAL *"paid but cannot send"* when a demo cannot send **by design**; the **double-grant** check matched a wallet balance alone, flagging a client credited by `manual_grant` who never paid us; and the **MBF demo** was matched on the exact string `MBF Holdings` while the live account is `MBF Demo`, so it reported *"does not exist"* about an account sitting in the client list — right conclusion, false reason, which is not a report.
